Proof.

Throughout the proof, fix and order
(\mathbin{\prec})\in(X_{1}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}X_{r})
.
For simplicity, we focus on the part of the statement that
builds a solution over
\mathbb{N}
(in fact, we will build a solution over
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
).
The fact that there are
infinitely many solutions follows from the
fact that the solution is built by solely relying on systems
of (non-)congruences over the integers.

Let us first expand on the overview of the proof given in
Section

1.3
by referring to the pseudocode in
Algorithm

1
.
The goal is to compute a map
\boldsymbol{\nu}\colon\big(\bigcup_{j=1}^{r}X_{j}\big)\to\mathbb{Z}_{+}
such that
\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x})
is a solution for
\Phi
. The proof proceeds by
induction on
r
, populating the map
\boldsymbol{\nu}
according
the order
\mathbin{\prec}
.

When
r=1
(line
6

in
Algorithm

1
)
\boldsymbol{\nu}
can be
computed using the (standard) Chinese remainder theorem,
with little to no problem (line
10
). The
main ingredient here is given by the natural number
\mu_{p}\coloneqq\max\big\{v_{p}(f(\boldsymbol{b}_{p})):f(\boldsymbol{x})\text{
left-hand side of a divisibility in }\Phi\}

(line
7
), that given
p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)
tells us up to what power of
p
should the
integer solution given by
\boldsymbol{\nu}
agree with the solution

\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
.

When
r\geq 2
, the
goal is to define
\boldsymbol{\nu}
for the variables in
X_{1}
in
such a way that the formula
\Phi^{\prime}\coloneqq\Phi[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x:x\in X_{1}]
is increasing
for
X_{2}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}X_{r}
, and has solutions
modulo
p
for every
p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi^{\prime})
. This allows us
to call for
Theorem

4
inductively
(line
27
), obtaining a solution
\boldsymbol{\xi}\colon\big(\bigcup_{j=2}^{r}X_{j}\big)\to\mathbb{Z}_{+}
for

\Phi^{\prime}
. An integer solution for
\Phi
is then given by the
union
\boldsymbol{\nu}\sqcup\boldsymbol{\xi}
of
\boldsymbol{\nu}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}
, i.e., the map defined as
\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)
for
x\in X_{1}

and as
\boldsymbol{\xi}(y)
for
y\in\bigcup_{j=2}^{r}X_{j}
,
(line
28
). To construct
\boldsymbol{\nu}
for

X_{1}
, we first close
\Phi
under the elimination property
following
Lemma

7
(line
14
),
and extend the solutions
\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
to every
p\in\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
thanks to
Lemma

3

(line
15
). We then populate
\boldsymbol{\nu}
following the order
\mathbin{\prec}
, starting from the
smallest
variable (line
20
). In the
proof, this is done with a second induction. Values for the
variables in
X_{1}
are found using
Theorem

3

(line
22
). When a new value
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
for a variable
x\in X_{1}
is found, new primes
need to be taken into account (line
24
),
since substituting
a
for
x
yields a complete evaluation
of the polynomials in
S(\Delta(\Phi))
with leading
variable
x
, and the resulting integers might be divisible by
primes not belonging to
\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
. For subsequent
variables in
X_{1}
, we make sure to pick values that keep
the evaluated polynomials as “coprime as possible” with
respect to these new primes (see the induction
hypothesis (
IH2:
) below, as well as the
system of
(non-)congruences
in
line
22
). This condition is
necessary to obtain the new solutions
\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
for the
formula
\Phi^{\prime}
, modulo every
p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi^{\prime})

(line
26
).

We now formalize the proof. To ease the presentation, we
postpone the analysis on the bound of the minimal positive
solution to after the main induction showing the existence
of such a solution. In a nutshell, the bound fundamentally
comes from repeated applications of
Theorem

3
.

Base case
r=1
:

As
\Phi
is
1
-increasing, it is of the form

{\bigwedge_{i=1}^{\ell}c_{i}\mathrel{|}g_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\land\bigwedge_{j=\ell+1}^{m}f_{j}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrel{|}a_{j}\cdot f_{j}(\boldsymbol{x})}
, where every
c_{i}
and
a_{j}
are in
\mathbb{Z}
. By
hypothesis, every
c_{i}
and
f_{j}
is non-zero. If
c_{i}=1

for every
i\in[1,\ell]
, then
\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{0}
is
trivially a solution. Otherwise,
\mathbb{P}(\Phi)
is
non-empty. Let
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})
and, given
p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)
, let
\mu_{p}\coloneqq\max\{v_{p}(f(\boldsymbol{b}_{p})):f\text{ is in the left-hand side of a divisibility of }\Phi\}
. Note that since
\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
is a solution for

\Phi
modulo
p
, we have
f_{j}(\boldsymbol{b}_{p})\neq 0
for every

j\in[\ell+1,m]
, and thus
v_{p}(f(\boldsymbol{b}_{p}))\in\mathbb{N}
.
Denote with
b_{p,k}
the value of
\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
for the
variable
x_{k}
, with
p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)
and
k\in[1,d]
.
Consider the system of congruences

\displaystyle x_{k}\equiv b_{p,k}

\displaystyle\pmod{p^{\mu_{p}+1}}

\displaystyle p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi),\ 1\leq k\leq d.

(4)

According to the Chinese remainder theorem, this system has
a positive solution
\boldsymbol{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{d})
. To conclude
the base case, it suffices to show that
f_{j}(\boldsymbol{a})\neq 0

for every
j\in[\ell+1,m]
, and that
c_{i}\mathrel{|}g_{i}(\boldsymbol{a})
for every
i\in[1,\ell]
. First, consider
j\in[\ell+1,m]
and pick a prime
p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)
. From the
system of congruences in
Equation

4
we have

f_{j}(\boldsymbol{a})\equiv f_{j}(\boldsymbol{b}_{p})\pmod{p^{\mu_{p}+1}}
, and
by definition of
\mu_{p}
,
f_{j}(\boldsymbol{b}_{p})\not\equiv 0\pmod{p^{\mu_{p}+1}}
. We conclude that
f_{j}(\boldsymbol{a})\not\equiv 0\pmod{p^{\mu_{p}+1}}
, and so
f_{j}(\boldsymbol{a})\neq 0
.

Consider now
i\in[1,\ell]
. To prove that
c_{i}\mathrel{|}g_{i}(\boldsymbol{a})
, concluding the base case, we show that for
every prime
p
dividing
c_{i}
,
{v_{p}(c_{i})\leq v_{p}(g_{i}(\boldsymbol{a}))}
. By definition, any such prime
p
satisfies
p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)
and moreover
{v_{p}(c_{i})\leq\mu_{p}}
. We
distinguish two cases:

•

if
v_{p}(g_{i}(\boldsymbol{b}_{p}))\leq\mu_{p}
, then according
to
Equation

4
we have
v_{p}(g_{i}(\boldsymbol{b}_{p}))=v_{p}(g_{i}(\boldsymbol{a}))
. Since
\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
is a solution for

\Phi
modulo
p
, this implies
v_{p}(c_{i})\leq v_{p}(g_{i}(\boldsymbol{a}))
.

•

If
v_{p}(g_{i}(\boldsymbol{b}_{p}))>\mu_{p}
, then
g_{i}(\boldsymbol{b}_{p})\equiv 0\pmod{p^{\mu_{p}+1}}
and so
g_{i}(\boldsymbol{a})\equiv 0\pmod{p^{\mu_{p}+1}}
by
Equation

4
.
Therefore
v_{p}(g_{i}(\boldsymbol{a}))>\mu_{p}
and by definition of

\mu_{p}
we get
v_{p}(c_{i})\leq v_{p}(g_{i}(\boldsymbol{a}))
.

Induction step
r\geq 2
:

by induction hypothesis, we assume the theorem to be true
for every
s
-increasing system with
s<r
.
By
Lemma

3
, for every prime
p\in\mathbb{P}\setminus\mathbb{P}(\Phi)
there is a solution
\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
for

\Phi
modulo
p
such that
\max\{v_{p}(f(\boldsymbol{b}_{p})):f\text{ in the left-hand side of a divisibility of }\Phi\}=0
. Together with the solutions
\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
for primes
p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)
, this means that
\Phi
has solutions
modulo every prime. We
apply
Lemma

7
in order to
obtain from
\Phi
a system
\Psi
with the elimination
property for
\mathbin{\prec}
. The system
\Psi
is used to produce
the map
\boldsymbol{\nu}
for the variables in
X_{1}
. Adding the
elimination property does not change the set of solutions
(neither over the integers nor modulo a prime), and
therefore the above solutions
\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
are still solutions
for
\Psi
modulo
p
. Below, among these solutions we only
consider the ones for primes
p\in\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
. Given
such a prime
p\in\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
, define
\mu_{p}\coloneqq\max\{v_{p}(f(\boldsymbol{b}_{p})):f\text{ is in the left-hand side
of a divisibility of }\Psi\}
. As already observed in the
base case, given
f
left-hand side of a divisibility in

\Psi
,
f(\boldsymbol{b}_{p})\neq 0
and so
v_{p}(f(\boldsymbol{b}_{p}))\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover, from
Item

1

in
Lemma

7
we conclude that

\mu_{p}=0
for every
p\in\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)\setminus\mathbb{P}(\Phi)
.

As
\Psi
is
r
-increasing
(see
Item

1

in
Lemma

7
), it is of the form

\left(\bigwedge_{i=1}^{\ell}c_{i}\mid g_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\right)\wedge\left(\bigwedge_{i=\ell+1}^{n}f_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\mid g_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})+g_{i}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})\right)\wedge\left(\bigwedge_{i=n+1}^{t}f_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})+f_{i}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})\mid g_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})+g_{i}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})\right),

(5)

where
\boldsymbol{x}
are the variables appearing in
X_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{y}
are the variables appearing in
\bigcup_{j=2}^{r}X_{j}
,

\ell\leq n\leq t
, and for every
i\in[n+1,t]
,

f_{i}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})
and
g_{i}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})
have
0
as a constant and
are non-constant. Moreover, since
\Psi
is increasing, for
every
i\in[\ell+1,n]

g_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})
and
g_{i}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})

are such that either
g_{i}^{\prime}=0
and
g_{i}=a\cdot f_{i}
for
some
a\in\mathbb{Z}
, or
g_{i}^{\prime}
is non-constant and has
0
as a
constant. Let
X_{1}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{d}\}
, with
x_{1}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}x_{d}
. Denote by
b_{p,k}
the value of

\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
for the variable
x_{k}
, with
p\in\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)

and
k\in[1,d]
. We build the map
\boldsymbol{\nu}
defined on
the variables in
X_{1}
, inductively starting from
x_{1}
. In
the induction step, when searching for a value to the
variable
x_{k+1}
, the following induction hypotheses hold:

IH1:

For every
p\in\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
and
j\in[1,k]
,
\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{j})\equiv b_{p,j}\pmod{p^{\mu_{p}+1}}
,

IH2:

For every prime
p\notin\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
, for every
h,h^{\prime}\in\Delta(\Psi)
with leading variable at most
x_{k}
, if

S(h,h^{\prime})
is not identically zero, then
p
does not
divide both
h(\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}))
and
h^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}))
.

IH3:

h(\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}))\neq 0
for every
h\in\Delta(\Psi)
that is non-zero and with
\textup{LV}(h)\preceq x_{k}
.

base case
k=0
.

In this case, (
IH1:
)
and (
IH3:
) trivially hold
(for (
IH3:
) note that
h
is
constant). In (
IH2:
) we only
consider constant polynomials
h,h^{\prime}
, hence
S(h,h^{\prime})=0
by definition.

induction step.

Let us assume that
\boldsymbol{\nu}
is
defined for the variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
with
k\in[0,d-1]
, so that the induction hypotheses hold. Let us
provide a value for
x_{k+1}
so that
\boldsymbol{\nu}
still
fulfils the induction hypotheses. We define the following
set of primes:

P_{k}\coloneqq\left\{p\in\mathbb{P}:p\in\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)\text{ or }p\mid h(\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}))\text{ for }h\in S(\Delta{(\Psi)})\backslash\{0\}\text{ with }\textup{LV}(h)\preceq x_{k}\right\}.

In the hypothesis that
P_{k}=\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
, we add to

P_{k}
the smallest prime not in
\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
. Hence,
below, assume
P_{k}\neq\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
. We consider the
following system of (non-)congruences:

\displaystyle x_{k+1}

\displaystyle\equiv b_{p,k+1}

\displaystyle\pmod{p^{\mu_{p}+1}}

\displaystyle p\in\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)

\displaystyle h(\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}),\,x_{k+1})

\displaystyle\not\equiv 0

\displaystyle\pmod{q}

\displaystyle q\in P_{k}\setminus\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)\text{ and}

\displaystyle h\in S(\Delta(\Psi))\text{ s.t.}~\textup{LV}(h)=x_{k+1}.

With respect to the
h
above, let us write
h(\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}),x_{k+1})=c_{h}+a_{h}\cdot x_{k+1}
,
where
c_{h}
is the constant term obtained by partially
evaluating
h
with respect to
\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
,
and
a_{h}
is the coefficient of
x_{k+1}
in
h
. Since
q\in P_{k}\setminus\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
, then
q\nmid a_{h}
from
Condition
(P2)
. Then
a_{h}
has an inverse

a_{h}^{-1}
modulo
q
, and the system of (non-)congruences
above is equivalent to

\displaystyle x_{k+1}

\displaystyle\equiv b_{p,k+1}

\displaystyle\pmod{p^{\mu_{p}+1}}

\displaystyle p\in\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)

(6)

\displaystyle x_{k+1}

\displaystyle\not\equiv-a_{h}^{-1}c_{h}

\displaystyle\pmod{q}

\displaystyle\hskip-10.0ptq\in P_{k}\setminus\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)\text{ and }h\in S(\Delta(\Psi))\text{ s.t.}~\textup{LV}(h)=x_{k+1}.

In this system of (non-)congruences, elements in

\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
and
P_{k}\setminus\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
are
pairwise coprime,
P_{k}\setminus\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
is a set of
primes, and moreover
\min(P_{k}\setminus\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi))>\#S(\Delta(\Psi))
by Condition
(P1)
.
Hence, we can apply
Theorem

3
and conclude
that
Equation

6
has a solution
w\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Let us update
\boldsymbol{\nu}
so that
\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{k+1})=w
. We show that
\boldsymbol{\nu}
satisfies the induction
hypotheses.

1.

By the congruences
in
Equation

6
,
\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{k+1})\equiv b_{p,k+1}\pmod{p^{\mu_{p}+1}}
,
hence (
IH1:
) holds.

2.

Consider
h,h^{\prime}\in\Delta(\Psi)
such that
\textup{LV}(h)\preceq\textup{LV}(h^{\prime})=x_{k+1}
and
S(h,h^{\prime})
is not
identically zero. Note that the case where
\textup{LV}(h^{\prime})\preceq\textup{LV}(h)=x_{k+1}
is analogous, whereas if
both
\textup{LV}(h)
and
\textup{LV}(h^{\prime})
are at most
x_{k}

then (
IH2:
) already holds by
induction hypothesis. We divide the proof into two
cases, depending on
\textup{LV}(h)
.

•

if
\textup{LV}(h)\mathbin{\prec}x_{k+1}
, consider
p\not\in\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
such that
p\mathrel{|}h(\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}))
. By definition,
p\in P_{k}
, and
thus from the non-congruences
in
Equation

6
,
p\nmid h(\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k+1}))
.

•

if
\textup{LV}(h)=\textup{LV}(h^{\prime})=x_{k+1}
, assume
ad
absurdum
that there is
p\not\in\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
such
that
p\mathrel{|}h(\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k+1}))
and
p\mathrel{|}h^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k+1}))
. Then,
p\mathrel{|}S(h,h^{\prime})
by definition of
S
. However,
S(h,h^{\prime})\in S(\Delta(\Psi))\setminus\{0\}
and
\textup{LV}(S(h,h^{\prime}))\preceq x_{k}
, from which we conclude that
p\in P_{k}
. Again from the non-congruences
in
Equation

6
, this implies
p\nmid h(\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k+1}))
and
p\nmid h^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k+1}))
, a contradiction.

In both cases, we conclude
that (
IH2:
) holds.

3.

Let
h\in\Delta(\Psi)
with
\textup{LV}(h)=x_{k+1}

(else (
IH3:
) directly holds by
induction hypothesis). As there is a prime
p\in P_{k}\setminus\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
, from the non-congruences
of
Equation

6
we conclude
p\nmid h(\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k+1}))
, and thus
h(\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k+1}))
cannot be
0
.
Hence, (
IH3:
) holds.

The innermost induction we have just completed yields a map

\boldsymbol{\nu}
defined for the variables in
X_{1}
and
satisfying (
IH1:
)–(
IH3:
)
for every
k\in[1,d]
. Consider the system
\Psi^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})\coloneqq\Psi[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x:x\in X_{1}]

obtained from
\Psi
by evaluating as
\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)
every
variable
x
in
X_{1}
. With reference
to
Equation

5
, we note that the
subsystem
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{\ell}c_{i}\mathrel{|}g_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}))
evaluates to true (proof as in the base case
r=1
of
the induction and by using (
IH1:
)).
Then,
\Psi^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})
is of the form

\left(\bigwedge_{i=\ell+1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\mid\beta_{i}+g_{i}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})\right)\wedge\left(\bigwedge_{i=n+1}^{t}\alpha_{i}+f_{i}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})\mid\beta_{i}+g_{i}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})\right),

(7)

where
\alpha_{i}=f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}))\in\mathbb{Z}
and

\beta_{i}=g_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}))\in\mathbb{Z}
, for every
i\in[\ell+1,t]
. Note that
\alpha_{i}\neq 0
for every
i\in[\ell+1,n]
, thanks to (
IH3:
),
so
\boldsymbol{\nu}
satisfies all trivial divisibilities of the
form
f(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrel{|}a\cdot f(\boldsymbol{x})
.

The next step is to show that
\Psi^{\prime}
is increasing for

(X_{2}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}X_{r})
and to provide solutions
modulo
p
for every
p\in\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi^{\prime})
. These two
properties, formalized below
in
4

and
5
, follow from the
induction
hypotheses (
IH1:
)–(

)
we kept during the construction of
\boldsymbol{\nu}
, together with
the fact that the system
\Psi
has the elimination
property. Their proofs are very technical and lengthy, and
we therefore defer them
to
Appendix

E
.
Observe that the condition
(P3)
of the difficult primes is required to establish

, but otherwise does not appear anywhere else in this proof.

Claim 4
.

The system
\Psi^{\prime}
is increasing for
(X_{2}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}X_{r})
.

Claim 5
.

For every
p\in\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
, the solution
\boldsymbol{b}_{p}

for
\Psi
modulo
p
is, when restricted to
\boldsymbol{y}
, a
solution for
\Psi^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})
modulo
p
. For every
prime
{p\not\in\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)}
, there is a solution

\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
for
\Psi^{\prime}
modulo
p
such that (i) every
entry of
\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
belongs to
{[0,p^{u+1}-1]}
, where

{u\coloneqq\max\{v_{p}(\alpha_{i}):i\in[\ell\,{+}\,1,n]\}}
, and (ii) for every
g\in\textup{terms}(\Psi^{\prime})
,
v_{p}(g(\boldsymbol{b}_{p}))
is either
0
or
u
.

Thanks to
4

and
5
, we can inductively apply
the statement of
Theorem

4
on
\Psi^{\prime}

in order to obtain an integer solution for
\Psi
, and thus
a solution for the original system
\Phi
. While this would
prove the local-to-global property, it is not enough to
obtain the upper bound on the size of the minimal positive
solution stated in
Theorem

4
. Instead,
we wish to apply the induction hypothesis on the system

\Phi^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})\coloneqq\Phi[\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathbin{/}x:x\in X_{1}]
, hence disregarding the work done to close

\Phi
under the elimination property. The main point in
favour of this strategy is that the subsequent applications
of
Lemma

7
, required to
inductively construct the integer solutions for the
remaining variables
\boldsymbol{y}
, yield smaller systems of
divisibility constraints (for instance, note that
\Phi^{\prime}
has at
most
m
divisibilities, whereas
\Psi^{\prime}
can have close to

m\cdot(d+2)
divisibilities).

To prove that we can apply the induction hypothesis on

\Phi^{\prime}
, we need to show that this system satisfies
properties analogous to the ones
in
4

and
5
. While the proofs of
these claims require the elimination property to be
established, we can transfer them to
\Phi^{\prime}
thanks to the
fact that
\Psi
is defined from
\Phi
following the
algorithm of
Lemma

7
.

Claim 6
.

The system
\Phi^{\prime}
is increasing for
(X_{2}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}X_{r})
.

Proof.

Ad absurdum
, assume that
\Phi^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})
is not
increasing for some order
(\mathbin{\prec}^{\prime})\in(X_{2}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}X_{r})
. Let
\boldsymbol{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{j})
with

y_{1}\mathbin{\prec}^{\prime}\dots\mathbin{\prec}^{\prime}y_{j}
. There is
i\in[1,j]

and a primitive term
f
with
\textup{LV}(f)=y_{i}
such that

\mathbb{Z}f\varsubsetneq\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi^{\prime})\cap\mathbb{Z}[y_{1},\dots,y_{i}]
. By
Lemma

8
we
get
\mathbb{Z}f\varsubsetneq\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi^{\prime})\cap\mathbb{Z}[y_{1},\dots,y_{i}]
. However, this implies that
\Psi^{\prime}
is
not increasing for
\mathbin{\prec}^{\prime}
,
contradicting
4
.
∎

Claim 7
.

For every
p\in\mathbb{P}
, the solution
\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
for
\Psi^{\prime}

modulo
p
ensured in
5
is
also a solution for
\Phi^{\prime}
modulo
p
. If
p\not\in\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
, then for every polynomial
f^{\prime}
appearing in
the left-hand side of a divisibility of
\Phi^{\prime}
, we have
either
v_{p}(f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{b}_{p}))=0
or
v_{p}(f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{b}_{p}))=\max\{v_{p}(\alpha_{i}):i\in[\ell\,{+}\,1,n]\}
.

Proof.

For the first statement of the claim, consider a solution

\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
for
\Psi^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})
modulo
p
(such as the one
ensured by
5
). From the
definition of
\Psi^{\prime}
, the tuple
(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{b}_{p})
is a solution for
\Psi(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})
modulo
p
.
Then, by
Lemma

7
,
(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{b}_{p})
is a solution for
\Phi(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})
modulo
p
; and so by definition of
\Phi^{\prime}
,

\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
is a solution for
\Phi^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})
modulo
p
.

The second statement of this claim follows
from
5
together with the
property (
1
) of

Lemma

7
, and
by definitions of
\Psi^{\prime}
and
\Phi^{\prime}
. In particular, for
every polynomial
f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})
occurring in a left-hand
side of a divisibility of
\Phi^{\prime}
, there is a polynomial

f(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})
occurring in a left-hand side of

\Phi
such that
f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})=f(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
. From (
1
) of

Lemma

7
,
f
occurs in a
left-hand side of
\Psi
and thus
f^{\prime}
occurs in a
left-hand side of
\Psi^{\prime}
. The statement then follows
by
5
.
∎

From
6

and
7
, and by
induction hypothesis, there is a map
\boldsymbol{\xi}\colon\big(\bigcup_{j=2}^{r}X_{j}\big)\to\mathbb{Z}_{+}
such that
\boldsymbol{\xi}(\boldsymbol{y})
is a solution for
\Phi^{\prime}
. Note that in
constructing
\boldsymbol{\xi}
we can rely on the order
\mathbin{\prec}

restricted to
\bigcup_{j=2}^{r}X_{j}
; since
\Phi^{\prime}
is
increasing for that order. Then, by definition of
\Phi^{\prime}
, a
positive integer solution for
\Phi
is given by the union

\boldsymbol{\nu}\sqcup\boldsymbol{\xi}
of
\boldsymbol{\nu}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}
.
This concludes the proof of existence of a solution. We now
study its bit length.

In what follows, let
\underline{O}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
be the minimal
positive integer greater or equal than
4
such that the map

{x\mapsto\underline{O}(x+1)}
upper bounds the linear
functions hidden in the
O(.)
appearing
in
Lemma

7
. We
write
\Gamma(r,\ell,w,m,d)
, with
r,\ell,w,m,d\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}

and
r\leq d
, for the maximum bit length of the minimal
positive solution of any system of divisibility constraints
\Phi

such that:

•

\Phi
is
r
-increasing.

•

The maximum bit length of a coefficient or constant
appearing in
\Phi
, i.e.,
\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle
, is at most

\ell
.

•

For every
p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)
, consider a solution

\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
of
\Phi
modulo
p
minimizing
\mu_{p}\coloneqq\max\{{v_{p}(f(\boldsymbol{b}_{p}))}:f\text{ is in the
left-hand side of a divisibility in }\Phi\}
. Then,

\log_{2}\left(\prod_{p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)}p^{\mu_{p}+1}\right)\leq w
.

•

\Phi
has at most
m
divisibilities.

•

\Phi
has at most
d
variables.

The constraint
r\leq d
is without loss of
generality, as every increasing formula is
d
-increasing.

Since we want to find an upper bound for
\Gamma
, assume
without loss of generality that
\Gamma(r,\ell,w,m,d)
is
always at least
\min(\ell,w)
.
In
Appendix

F
we study the growth
of
\Gamma
and prove the following claim.

Claim 8
.

\begin{cases}\Gamma(1,\ell,w,m,d)\leq w+3\\
\Gamma(r+1,\ell,w,m,d)\leq\begin{aligned} \Gamma(&r,\\
&2^{105}m^{27}(d+2)^{38}\underline{O}\cdot\log_{2}(\underline{O})^{6}(\ell+w)\cdot(\log_{2}(\ell+w))^{6},\\
&2^{109}m^{29}(d+2)^{39}\underline{O}\cdot\log_{2}(\underline{O})^{6}(\ell+w)\cdot(\log_{2}(\ell+w))^{6},\\
&m,\\
&d).\end{aligned}\end{cases}

Let us show that the recurrence system above yields the
bound in the statement of the theorem. Remark that
\Gamma
is
monotonous by definition. By induction on
k\in[0,r-1]
we
show that

\Gamma(r,\ell,w,m,d)\leq\Gamma(r-k,\delta_{k},\delta_{k},m,d)\text{ where }\delta_{k}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\cdot(2^{110}m^{29}(d+2)^{39}\underline{O}\cdot\log_{2}(\underline{O})^{6}(\ell+w))^{2(k+1)}.

base case
k=0
.

Directly follows from
\delta_{0}\geq\max(\ell,w)
and the fact that
\Gamma
is
monotonous.

induction case
k\geq 1
.

Let us define
C\coloneqq 2^{110}m^{29}(d+2)^{39}\underline{O}\cdot\log_{2}(\underline{O})^{6}
. By
induction hypothesis,
\Gamma(r,\ell,w,m,d)\leq\Gamma(r-(k-1),\delta_{k-1},\delta_{k-1},m,d)
.
By
8

and the monotonicity of
\Gamma
:

\displaystyle{\Gamma(r-(k-1),\delta_{k-1},\delta_{k-1},m,d)}

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\Gamma(r-k,\ \frac{C}{2}\cdot(2\cdot\delta_{k-1})\cdot(\log_{2}(2\cdot\delta_{k-1}))^{6},\ \frac{C}{2}\cdot(2\cdot\delta_{k-1})\cdot(\log_{2}(2\cdot\delta_{k-1}))^{6},\ m,\ d)

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\Gamma(r-k,\ \delta_{k},\ \delta_{k}\ m,\ d),

as indeed

\displaystyle\frac{C}{2}\cdot(2\cdot\delta_{k-1})\cdot(\log_{2}(2\cdot\delta_{k-1}))^{6}

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\frac{C}{2}\cdot\big(C\cdot(\ell+w)\big)^{2k}\big(\log_{2}((C\cdot(\ell+w))^{2k})\big)^{6}

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\frac{C}{2}\cdot\big(C\cdot(\ell+w)\big)^{2k}(2\cdot k)^{6}\log_{2}(C\cdot(\ell+w))^{6}

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\frac{C}{2}\cdot\big(C\cdot(\ell+w)\big)^{2k}\cdot\sqrt{C}\cdot\log_{2}(C\cdot(\ell+w))^{6}

from
k<r\leq d
and
(2\cdot d)^{6}\leq\sqrt{C}

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\frac{C}{2}\cdot\big(C\cdot(\ell+w)\big)^{2k}\cdot\sqrt{C}\cdot\sqrt{C\cdot(\ell+w)}

from
\log_{2}(x)^{6}\leq\sqrt{x}
for
x\geq 2^{75}

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot\big(C\cdot(\ell+w)\big)^{2(k+1)}=\delta_{k}.

The inequality we just showed, together with the base case
of the recurrence system, entails

\Gamma(r,\ell,w,m,d)\leq(2^{110}m^{29}(d+2)^{39}\underline{O}\cdot\log_{2}(\underline{O})^{6}(\ell+w))^{2\cdot r}.

(8)

Take now the formula
\Phi
in the statement of the theorem.
This formula belongs to
\Gamma(r,\ell,w,m,d)
where
\ell\coloneqq\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle
and
w\coloneqq\log_{2}\big(\prod_{p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)}p^{\mu_{p}+1}\big)
. We have

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\leq\max\{1+v_{p}(f(\boldsymbol{b}_{p})):f\text{ is in a left-hand side of }\Phi,\,p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)\}\cdot\log_{2}\Big(\prod_{p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)}p\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\max\{\langle f(\boldsymbol{b}_{p})\rangle:f\text{ is in a left-hand side of }\Phi,\,p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)\}\cdot\log_{2}\Big(\prod_{p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)}p\Big)

\displaystyle\leq(\max\{\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{b}_{p}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle:p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)\}+\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle+d+1)\cdot\log_{2}\Big(\prod_{p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)}p\Big)

\displaystyle\leq(\max\{\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{b}_{p}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle:p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)\}+\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle+d+1)\cdot m^{2}(d+2)\cdot(\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle+2)

\displaystyle\leq(\max\{\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{b}_{p}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle:p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)\}+1)\cdot m^{2}(d+2)^{2}(\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle+2)^{2}.

Then, following
Equation

8
,
the minimal positive solution of
\Phi
is bounded by

\displaystyle\left(2^{111}\underline{O}\cdot\log_{2}(\underline{O})^{6}m^{31}(d+2)^{41}(\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle+2)^{2}(\max\{\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{b}_{p}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle:p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)\}+2)\right)^{2r},

which is in
(\langle\Phi\rangle+\max\{\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{b}_{p}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle:p\in\mathbb{P}(\Phi)\})^{O(r)}
.
∎

Remark 1
.

Let us briefly discuss how the infinitely many solutions
of
\Phi
ensured by
Theorem

4
look
like. Since solutions are constructed by solving the
systems of (non-)congruences
in
Equations

4
and
6

(see
Algorithm

1
for a summary),

Theorem

3
ensures that
\Phi
has infinitely
many solutions. More precisely, the following property
holds: let
(\mathbin{\prec})\in(X_{1}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}X_{r})
,
{x\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{r}X_{j}}
, and
\boldsymbol{\nu}\colon\bigcup_{j=1}^{r}X_{j}\to\mathbb{Z}
be the solution of
\Phi

computed by
Algorithm

1
. The system

{\Phi[\boldsymbol{\nu}(y)\mathbin{/}y:y\mathbin{\prec}x]}
has a
solution for infinitely many positive and negative values
of
x
.

3.3
Deciding systems of divisibility constraints in increasing form in NP

Theorem

4
provides a way of
constructing integer solutions of bit length exponential in

r
for
r
-increasing
systems of divisibility
constraints. A different approach not relying on
constructing integer solutions shows that the feasibility
problem for systems of divisibility constraints in
increasing form is in
NP
.

Let
\Phi(\boldsymbol{x})\coloneqq\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}\mathrel{|}g_{i}

be a formula in increasing form for an order
\mathbin{\prec}
.
According to
Theorem

4
,
\Phi
is
satisfiable over the integers if and only if there are
solutions
\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
for
\Phi
modulo
p
for every prime

p
belonging to
\mathbb{P}(\Phi)
.
From
Lemma

4
, the bit length of

\mathbb{P}(\Phi)
is polynomial in
\langle\Phi\rangle
, and
therefore only polynomially many primes of polynomial bit
length need to be considered. Recall that
\Phi
has a
solution modulo
p
whenever the system
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}v_{p}(f_{i}(\boldsymbol{x}))\leq v_{p}(g_{i}(\boldsymbol{x}))\land f_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\neq 0
has a solution. In
[
6
]
it is shown
that the feasibility problem for these constraint systems is
in
NP
(this result holds for solutions over the integers,

p
-adic integers, and
p
-adic numbers), and therefore
there are certificates of feasibility having size polynomial
in
\langle p\rangle
and
\langle\Phi\rangle
. The set of these
certificates, one for each prime in
\mathbb{P}(\Phi)
, is a
polynomial size certificate for the feasibility of
\Phi
.

Proposition 2
.

Feasibility for systems of divisibility constraints in
increasing form is in
NP
.

Of course, we know from the family of formulae
\Phi_{n}

introduced in
Section

1.1
(and the one
after
Theorem

4
) that systems in
increasing form might have minimal solutions of exponential
bit length. Therefore,
Proposition

2
is of no
use when establishing
Theorem

1
. However, it
still has an interesting implication: if the feasibility
problem for systems of divisibility constraints lies outside

NP
, then there is no polynomial time non-deterministic
Turing machine for finding an equisatisfiable system in
increasing form.

4
IP-GCD systems have polynomial size solutions

In this section we expand the summary
provided
Section

1.4
and
establish
Theorem

1
, i.e., that every feasible
IP-GCD system has solutions of polynomial bit length, and
that this polynomial bound still holds when looking at
minimization or maximization of linear objectives. As
explained in
Section

1.4
,
we prove
Theorem

1
by designing an algorithm
that reduces an IP-GCD system into a disjunction of
(exponentially many)
3
-increasing systems of
divisibility constraints with coefficients and constants of polynomial
size, to then study bounds on their solutions modulo primes.
Then, the polynomial small witness property follows
from
Theorem

4
.

Without loss of generality, throughout the section we
consider IP-GCD systems of the form

A\cdot\boldsymbol{x}\leq\boldsymbol{b}\land\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}\gcd(y_{i},z_{i})\sim_{i}c_{i}\,,

where,
A\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times d}
,
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
,
c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})
is a vector of variables,

\sim_{i}{}\in\{{\leq},{=},{\neq},

{\geq}\}
, and the

y_{i}
and
z_{i}
are variables occurring in
\boldsymbol{x}
. Systems
with GCD constraints
\gcd(f(\boldsymbol{w}),g(\boldsymbol{w}))\sim c
can
be put into this form by replacing
\gcd(f(\boldsymbol{w}),g(\boldsymbol{w}))\sim c
with
y=f(\boldsymbol{w})\land z=g(\boldsymbol{w})\land\gcd(y,z)\sim c
, where
y
and
z
are fresh variables.

4.1
Translation into 3-increasing systems

The procedure generating the
3
-increasing systems of
divisibility constraints starting from an IP-GCD system
\Phi
is
divided into two steps: we first (
Algorithm

2
) compute several
systems of divisibility constraints whose disjunction is equivalent
to
\Phi
(under some changes of variables). We now
describe these two steps in detail, and study bounds on the
obtained
3
-increasing formulae
(
Lemma

13
).
Both steps
rely on the following notion of
gcd-to-div
triple, which
highlights properties of the system of divisibility constraints
obtained by translation from IP-GCD systems. A triple

(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)
is said to be a
gcd-to-div

triple
whenever there are
d,m\in\mathbb{N}
and three disjoint families
of variables
\boldsymbol{z}
,
\boldsymbol{y}
and
\boldsymbol{w}
for which the following properties hold:

1.

\Psi(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{w})
is a system of divisibility constraints in
m
variables,

\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
E\in\mathbb{Z}^{d\times m}
, where
each column of
E
(implicitly) corresponds to a variable
in
\Psi
.

2.

Each divisibility in

\Psi
is of the form
h(\boldsymbol{z})\mathrel{|}f(\boldsymbol{y})
or of the
form
f(\boldsymbol{y})\mathrel{|}g(\boldsymbol{w})
, with
g
being a
non-constant polynomial. Each polynomial only features
non-negative coefficients and constants, and each
left-hand side of a divisibility has a (strictly) positive
constant.

3.

In
\Psi
, each
variable in
\boldsymbol{z}
appears in a single
polynomial
h(\boldsymbol{z})
, where
h(\boldsymbol{z})
is of the form

z+c
, for some
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and occurs in precisely
two divisibilities (as left-hand side).

4.

In
\Psi
, each
variable in
\boldsymbol{w}
appears in exactly two polynomials

g_{1}(\boldsymbol{w})
and
g_{2}(\boldsymbol{w})
, each occurring in

\Psi
exactly once (as right-hand sides). They have the
form
g_{1}(\boldsymbol{w})=w
and
g_{2}(\boldsymbol{w})=w+c
, for some

c\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.

5.

Every column in
E

corresponding to a variable in
\boldsymbol{z}
or
\boldsymbol{w}
is
zero (see line
13

of
Algorithm

2
).

For a set of
gcd-to-div
triples
S
, let
\llbracket{S}\rrbracket\coloneqq\{\boldsymbol{u}+E\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda}\,:\,{(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)\in B}

\text{and }\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}\text{
solution to }\Psi\}
.

Step I: from IP-GCD to systems of divisibility constraints.

Algorithm 2

Translate a IP-GCD system into
gcd-to-div
triples

1:

An IP-GCD system
\Phi(\boldsymbol{x})=A\cdot\boldsymbol{x}\leq\boldsymbol{b}\land\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}\gcd(y_{i},z_{i})\sim_{i}c_{i}
with
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})
.

2:

A finite set
B
of
gcd-to-div
triples
satisfying
\{\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\boldsymbol{a}\text{ solution to }\Phi\}=\llbracket{B}\rrbracket
.

3:

G

\coloneqq

\{\Psi_{1}(\boldsymbol{x}),\dots,\Psi_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{x})\}
such that
\Phi
is equivalent to
\bigvee_{i=1}^{\ell}\Psi_{i}
and every
\Psi\in G
is a IP-GCD

system in which every GCD constraint
\gcd(y,z)\sim c
is such that (i) for both
w\in\{y,z\}

either
w\leq-1
or
w\geq 1
appear in
\Psi
, and (ii) the relation
\sim
is either
=
or
\geq

4:

B\coloneqq\emptyset

\triangleright

Set to be returned by the procedure

5:

for

\Psi

in

G

do

6:

\Psi^{\prime}

\coloneqq
linear inequalities in
\Psi

7:

S\coloneqq\{(\boldsymbol{u}_{i},E_{i}):i\in I\}
s.t.
\bigcup_{i\in I}\{\boldsymbol{u}_{i}+E_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{y}:\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\}
solutions set of
\Psi^{\prime}

\triangleright

Proposition

3

8:

for

(\boldsymbol{u},E)

in

S

do

9:

\Psi^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq

system of GCD constraints obtained from
\Psi
by performing the change of

variables
\boldsymbol{x}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{u}+E\cdot\boldsymbol{y}
, where
\boldsymbol{y}
is a vector of fresh variables (over
\mathbb{N}
)

10:

replace every polynomial
f
in
\Psi^{\prime\prime}
having only negative coefficients or constant with
-f

11:

replace every constraint
\gcd(f,g)=c
in
\Psi^{\prime\prime}
with

(c\mathrel{|}f)\land(c\mathrel{|}g)\land(f\mathrel{|}w)\land(g\mathrel{|}w+c)
,

where
w
is a fresh variable
(distinct GCD constraints gets distinct fresh variables)

12:

replace every constraint
\gcd(f,g)\geq c
in
\Psi^{\prime\prime}
with

(z+c\mathrel{|}f)\land(z+c\mathrel{|}g)
,

where
z
is a fresh variable
(distinct GCD constraints gets distinct fresh variables)

13:

add to
B
the triple
(\Psi^{\prime\prime},\boldsymbol{u},E^{\prime})
where
E^{\prime}
is obtained form
E
by adding a zero column for each auxiliary variable
z
and
w
added in lines
11
and
12

14:

return

B

This step is implemented
by
Algorithm

2
.
As highlighted in its signature, given as input an IP-GCD
system
\Phi(\boldsymbol{x})
having
d
variables and
k

GCD constraints, this procedure returns a set
B

of
gcd-to-div
triples satisfying the equivalence
\{\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\boldsymbol{a}\text{ solution to }\Phi\}=\llbracket{B}\rrbracket
.
This equivalence clarifies the role of the vector
\boldsymbol{u}

and matrix
E
of a
gcd-to-div
triple
(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)
:
they are used to perform a change of variables between the
variables
(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{w})
in
\Psi
and the
variables
\boldsymbol{x}
in
\Phi
. Note that, according to the
definition of
\llbracket{B}\rrbracket
, the values of
(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{w})
range over
\mathbb{N}
instead of
\mathbb{Z}
. This discrepancy
stems from the use of the forthcoming
Proposition

3
.

Let us discuss
how
Algorithm

2

computes
B
. As a preliminary step, the procedure computes
the formula
\bigvee_{i=1}^{\ell}\Psi_{i}
in
line
3
. The role of this formula
is to reduce the problem of translating IP-GCD systems into
systems of divisibility constraints to only those systems in which the
GCD constraints
\gcd(y,z)\leq c
and
\gcd(y,z)\neq c
do
not appear, and given a GCD constraint
\gcd(y,z)\sim c

(with
\sim
either
=
or
\geq
), the variables
y
and

z
are forced to be positive or negative (in particular,
non-zero). The formula
\bigvee_{i=1}^{\ell}\Psi_{i}
can be
computed from
\Phi
by opportunely applying the following
tautologies:

\displaystyle y\leq-1\lor y=0\lor y\geq 1\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-113.81102pt\gcd(y,z)\neq c+2\iff\gcd(y,z)\leq c+1\lor\gcd(y,z)\geq c+3\ \ (c\in\mathbb{N})\,,

\displaystyle\gcd(y,z)\neq 1\iff y=z=0\lor\gcd(y,z)\geq 2\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-426.79134pt\gcd(y,z)\leq c\iff\bigvee_{j=1}^{c}\gcd(y,z)=j\,,

\displaystyle y=0\implies(\gcd(y,z)\sim c\iff|z|\sim c)\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-426.79134pty\neq 0\land z=0\implies(\gcd(y,z)\sim c\iff|y|\sim c)\,,

where in the last two tautologies
\sim
is
=
or
\geq
,
and
|x|\sim c\,\coloneqq\,(x\geq 0\land x\sim c)\lor(x<0\land-x\sim c)
. Let
G\coloneqq\{\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{\ell}\}
(as defined in
line
3
). The next step of the
algorithm is to remove the system of inequalities from every
formula
\Psi\in G
via changes of variables
(lines
6
–
9
).
This can be done thanks to a fundamental result by von zur
Gathen and Sieveking
[
25
]
that characterises the
set of solutions of linear inequalities as a union of
discrete shifted cones. The following formulation of this
result is from
[
12
, Theorem 3]
.

Proposition 3

(
[
25
]
)
.

Consider matrices
A\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times d}
,
C\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times d}
, and vectors
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
,
\boldsymbol{d}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Let
r\coloneqq\textup{rank}(A)
and
s\coloneqq\textup{rank}{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A\\
C\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
. Then,

\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:A\cdot\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{b}\land C\cdot\boldsymbol{x}\leq\boldsymbol{d}\}\,=\,\bigcup_{i\in I}\{\boldsymbol{u}_{i}+E_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{y}\,:\,\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{N}^{d-r}\}\,,

where
I
is a finite set,
\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
E_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d\times(d-r)}
​ and
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}{|\mkern-2.0mu|},{|\mkern-2.0mu|}E_{i}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq(d+1)(s\cdot\max(2,{|\mkern-2.0mu|}A{|\mkern-2.0mu|},{|\mkern-2.0mu|}C{|\mkern-2.0mu|},{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{b}{|\mkern-2.0mu|},{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{d}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}))^{s}
.

Let
S=\{(\boldsymbol{u}_{i},E_{i}):i\in I\}
be the set of pairs
given by
Proposition

3
on the linear inequalities
of
\Psi
, as written in line
7
,
and given
(\boldsymbol{u},E)\in S
consider the system
\Psi^{\prime\prime}

defined in line
9
.
Following
Proposition

3
,
\Psi^{\prime\prime}
is interpreted over

\mathbb{N}
. By definition of
G
, in
\Psi
, every variable
x

appearing in a GCD constraint also appears in a (non-zero)
sign constraint
x\leq-1
or
x\geq 1
. This means that
in the system
\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{u}+E\cdot\boldsymbol{y}
, the row
corresponding to
x
is of the form
x=f(\boldsymbol{y})
where

f
is a linear polynomial having coefficients and constant
with the same polarity, i.e., they are all negatives (if
x\leq-1
) or positives (if
x\geq 1
). Therefore, all
GCD constraints in
\Psi^{\prime\prime}
are of the form
\gcd(f,g)\sim c
where
f
and
g
are polynomials with coefficients and
constant having the same polarity.
Line
10
modifies

\Psi^{\prime\prime}
so that every polynomial in it becomes of positive
polarity, thanks to the equalities
\gcd(f,g)=\gcd(-f,g)

and
\gcd(f,g)=\gcd(g,f)
.
What is left is to translate
\Psi^{\prime\prime}
into a system of
divisibilities. This is done in
lines
11

and
12
by simply relying
on the following two tautologies:

\displaystyle\gcd(f,g)=c\land f\neq 0\land g\neq 0

\displaystyle\iff\exists w\in\mathbb{N}:~c\mathrel{|}f~\land~c\mathrel{|}g~\land~f\mathrel{|}w~\land~g\mathrel{|}w+c\,,

(9)

\displaystyle\gcd(f,g)\geq c

\displaystyle\iff\exists z\in\mathbb{N}:~z+c\mathrel{|}f~\land~z+c\mathrel{|}g.

Above, note that we can assume
f\neq 0\land g\neq 0
in

\Psi^{\prime\prime}
, again because of the sign constraints appearing in

\Psi
. While the second tautology should be
self-explanatory, the first one merits a formal proof:

\displaystyle\gcd(f,g)=c\land f\neq 0\land g\neq 0

\displaystyle\iff\,

\displaystyle\exists a,b\in\mathbb{Z}:~c\mathrel{|}f~\land~c\mathrel{|}g~\land~a\cdot f+b\cdot g=c

Bézout’s
identity

\displaystyle\iff\,

\displaystyle\exists w,z\in\mathbb{Z}:\,w\leq 0\,\land\,c\mathrel{|}f\,\land\,c\mathrel{|}g\,\land\,f\mathrel{|}w\,\land\,g\mathrel{|}z\,\land\,w+z=c

set
w=a\cdot f
and
z=b\cdot g

Bézout’s identity allows picking
w\leq 0

\displaystyle\iff\,

\displaystyle\exists w\in\mathbb{Z}:\,w\leq 0\,\land\,c\mathrel{|}f\,\land\,c\mathrel{|}g\,\land\,f\mathrel{|}-w\,\land\,g\mathrel{|}c-w

eliminate
z
, and
f\mathrel{|}w\Leftrightarrow f\mathrel{|}-w

\displaystyle\iff\,

\displaystyle\exists w\in\mathbb{N}:\,c\mathrel{|}f\,\land\,c\mathrel{|}g\,\land\,f\mathrel{|}w\,\land\,g\mathrel{|}w+c

change of variable
-w\leftarrow w
.

Note that the divisibilities in (
9
)
ensure that
\Psi^{\prime\prime}
satisfies the constraints required
by
gcd-to-div
triples. After translating GCDs into
divisibilities, the procedure computes a matrix
E^{\prime}
by
enriching
E
with zero columns corresponding to the new
variables
z
and
w
, and adds the resulting triple

(\Psi^{\prime\prime},\boldsymbol{u},E^{\prime})
to
B

(line
13
). We obtain the
following result:

Lemma 10
.

Algorithm

2

respects its specification. Given as input a system
\Phi

with
d
variables and
k
GCD constraints, every triple

(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)
in the output set
B
is such that

\Psi
has at most
d+k
variables and
4k

divisibilities,
E
has at most
d
non-zero columns,
and
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Psi{|\mkern-2.0mu|},{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{u}{|\mkern-2.0mu|},{|\mkern-2.0mu|}E{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq(d+1)^{d+2}({|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+1)^{d+1}
​.

Proof.

The fact that

Algorithm

2

respects its specification follows from the discussion
given above. In particular,
\{\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\boldsymbol{a}\text{ solution of }\Phi\}=\llbracket{B}\rrbracket
stems from the fact that the
procedure treats the original formula
\Phi
by only
relying on tautologies and on
Proposition

3
.

Let us study the bounds on
(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)
. For the
bound on the number of variables in
\Psi
and non-zero
columns in
E
, note that by
Proposition

3
, the change
of variables of
line
9
does not
increase the number of variables, and therefore the only
lines where the number of variables increases are
lines
11

and
12
. Overall, these
two lines introduce
k
many variables, one for each
GCD constraint; so the number of variables in
\Psi
is
bounded by
d+k
. Each new variable introduces a zero
column in
E
, which has thus at most
d
non-zero columns
(line
13
). For the bound on
the number of divisibilities, only
lines
11

and
12
matter, and
they introduce at most
4
divisibilities per
GCD constraint; hence
\Psi
has at most
4k

divisibilities. Lastly, let us derive the bound on the
infinity norm of
\Psi
,
\boldsymbol{u}
and
E
. The rewritings
done in line
3
increase the
infinity norm by at most
1
; this occurs when relying on
the tautology
\gcd(y,z)\neq c+2\iff\gcd(y,z)\leq c+1\lor\gcd(y,z)\geq c+3
. The bound on
\boldsymbol{u}
and
E

then follows from a simple application
of
Proposition

3
:
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{u}{|\mkern-2.0mu|},{|\mkern-2.0mu|}E{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq(d+1)\cdot(d\cdot({|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+1))^{d}
. The change of
variables in
line
9
yields

{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Psi^{\prime\prime}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq(d+1)\cdot\max({|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{u}{|\mkern-2.0mu|},{|\mkern-2.0mu|}E{|\mkern-2.0mu|})\cdot({|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+1)
.
Lines
10
–
13

do not change the infinity norm, and therefore we obtain
the bound in the statement of the lemma.
∎

Step II: force increasingness.

We now move
to
Algorithm

3
, whose
role is to translate the systems of divisibility constraints computed
by
Algorithm

2

into
3
-increasing systems. As such, the procedure takes as
input a set
B
of
gcd-to-div
triples. We first need the
following result:

Algorithm 3

Translates the systems in
gcd-to-div
triples into
3
-increasing form

1:

A finite set
B
of
gcd-to-div
triples.

2:

A finite set
C
of
gcd-to-div
triples such that
\llbracket{B}\rrbracket=\llbracket{C}\rrbracket

and for every
(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)\in C
,
\Psi
is a
3
-increasing system of divisibility constraints.

3:

C\coloneqq\emptyset

\triangleright

Set to be returned by the procedure

4:

while

(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)\leftarrow\text{pop}(B)

do

\triangleright

exits when
B
becomes empty

5:

if

\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)\cap\mathbb{Z}=\{0\}
for every non-constant
f
primitive part of some l.h.s. in
\Psi

then

6:

add to
C
the triple
(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)

\triangleright

\Psi
in increasing form

7:

else

8:

f\coloneqq
non-constant primitive part of some l.h.s. in
\Psi
, satisfying
\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)\cap\mathbb{Z}\neq\{0\}

9:

\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{j}\coloneqq
the variables appearing in
f

10:

c\coloneqq
minimum positive integer in
\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)

11:

for

\boldsymbol{\nu}\colon\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{j}\}\to[0,c]
such that
f(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\lambda_{1}),\dots,\boldsymbol{\nu}(\lambda_{j}))
divides
c

do

12:

\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\coloneqq\Psi[\boldsymbol{\nu}(\lambda_{i})\mathbin{/}\lambda_{i}:i\in[1,j]]

\triangleright

\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
has fewer variables than
\Psi

13:

\boldsymbol{u}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{u}+\sum_{i=1}^{j}\boldsymbol{\nu}(\lambda_{i})\cdot\boldsymbol{p}_{i}
where
\boldsymbol{p}_{i}
is the column of
E
corresponding to the
variable
\lambda_{i}

14:

E_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\coloneqq

E
without the columns corresponding to
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{j}

15:

add to
B
the triple
(\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\boldsymbol{u}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}},E_{\boldsymbol{\nu}})

\triangleright

triple to be considered again in line
4

16:

return

C

Lemma 11
.

Let
(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)
be a
gcd-to-div
triple. If the
system
\Psi
is not in increasing form, then there is a
non-constant polynomial
f
primitive part of a left-hand
side in
\Psi
such that
\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)\cap\mathbb{Z}\neq\{0\}
. If
\Psi
is in increasing form, then it is
increasing for
\boldsymbol{z}\mathbin{\prec}\boldsymbol{y}\mathbin{\prec}\boldsymbol{w}
,
where
\boldsymbol{z}
,
\boldsymbol{y}
and
\boldsymbol{w}
are the families of
variables appearing in the definition of
gcd-to-div
triple.

Proof.

For the first statement, we prove a stronger result: if

\Psi
is not increasing for
\boldsymbol{z}\mathbin{\prec}\boldsymbol{y}\mathbin{\prec}\boldsymbol{w}
, then there is a non-constant
polynomial
f
primitive part of a left-hand side in

\Psi
s.t.
{\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)\cap\mathbb{Z}\neq\{0\}}
.
Observe that then, by definition of divisibility module
and increasing form,
\Psi
cannot be in increasing form
for any order; which shows the second statement in the
lemma by contrapositive.

Consider an order
x_{1}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}x_{d}
of the
variables in
\Psi
that belongs to
\boldsymbol{z}\mathbin{\prec}\boldsymbol{y}\mathbin{\prec}\boldsymbol{w}
, and suppose that
\Psi
is not in
increasing form for this order. Therefore, there is a
primitive part
f
of a left-hand side of a divisibility
in
\Psi
such that
\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)\cap\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\dots,x_{j}]\neq\mathbb{Z}f
, where
x_{j}=\textup{LV}(f)
. Let

g\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)\cap\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\dots,x_{j}]\setminus\mathbb{Z}f
. We show that
g
must be a constant polynomial. We
distinguish two cases, depending on whether the leading
variable of
f
belongs to
\boldsymbol{z}
or to
\boldsymbol{y}
(note
that it cannot belong to
\boldsymbol{w}
, as no left-hand sides
with variables from this family exists).

case
\textup{LV}(f)
is in
\boldsymbol{z}
.

Since

\textup{LV}(g)\preceq\textup{LV}(f)
, all variables in
g
are from

\boldsymbol{z}
. By Property
2

of
gcd-to-div
triple, each divisibility in
\Psi
is of
the form
h(\boldsymbol{z})\mathrel{|}h^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})
or of the form

h(\boldsymbol{y})\mathrel{|}h^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{w})
.
By
Lemma

6
, a set spanning

\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)
is given by
\{f,c_{1}\cdot g_{1},\dots,c_{m}\cdot g_{m}\}
where
c_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
and
g_{i}
is a
right-hand side of a divisibility in
\Psi
, for every

i\in[1,m]
. This means that every
g_{i}
has variables
from
\boldsymbol{y}
or
\boldsymbol{w}
. Since
g
does not have any
variable from
\boldsymbol{y}
or
\boldsymbol{w}
and belongs to
\mathbb{Z}f
, we conclude that it must be a constant polynomial.

case
\textup{LV}(f)
is in
\boldsymbol{y}
.

Again
from Property
2
of
gcd-to-div
triple,
f
only appears as left-hand side in
divisibilities of the form
a\cdot f(\boldsymbol{y})\mathrel{|}h(\boldsymbol{w})
, with
a\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
. Since no
non-constant polynomial
h(\boldsymbol{w})
appears in a
left-hand side of
\Psi
, the set
\{f,c_{1}\cdot g_{1},\dots,c_{m}\cdot g_{m}\}
spanning
\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)

computed via
Lemma

6
is such that
c_{i}\neq 0
if and only if
g_{i}
only has variables from

\boldsymbol{w}
, for every
i\in[1,m]
. Since
\mathbin{\prec}

belongs to
\boldsymbol{z}\mathbin{\prec}\boldsymbol{y}\mathbin{\prec}\boldsymbol{w}
, from

\textup{LV}(g)\mathbin{\prec}\textup{LV}(f)
we then conclude that
g
must
be a constant polynomial.∎

Consider
(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)\in B
.

Algorithm

3
relies on

Lemma

11
to test whether
\Psi
is
increasing
(line
5
). If it
is not, it computes the minimum positive integer
c\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)
, for some
f
non-constant
(line
10
). By definition
of divisibility module, for every primitive polynomial
f

and polynomial
g\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)
, we have that
\Psi

entails
f\mathrel{|}g
, that is for every
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}

solution to
\Psi
,
f(\boldsymbol{a})
divides
g(\boldsymbol{a})
; and
therefore
\Psi
entails
f\mathrel{|}c
. We can now eliminate
all variables that occur in
f
: by definition of
gcd-to-div
triple,
f
has coefficients and constant that are all
positive, and
\Psi
is interpreted over
\mathbb{N}
. We conclude
that every solution of
\Psi
is such that it assigns an
integer in
[0,c]
to every variable in
f
. The

for
loop in
line
11
iterates over the
subset of these (partial) assignments satisfying
f\mathrel{|}c
.
Each of these assignments
\boldsymbol{\nu}
yields a new triple

(\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\boldsymbol{u}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}},E_{\boldsymbol{\nu}})
,
defined as in
lines
12
–
14
,
which is a
gcd-to-div
triple thanks to the lemma below (that
follows directly from the definition of
gcd-to-div
triple).

Lemma 12
.

Let
(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)
be a
gcd-to-div
triple, with
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, and
X
be a subset of the variables appearing
in left-hand sides of
\Psi
. Consider a map
\boldsymbol{\nu}\colon X\to\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\Psi^{\prime}\coloneqq\Psi[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x:x\in X]
,
\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, and
E^{\prime}
be the matrix obtained from
E
by
removing the columns corresponding to variables in
X
.
The triple
(\Psi^{\prime},\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime},E^{\prime})
is a
gcd-to-div
triple.

The key equivalence, from which the correctness of the
algorithm directly stems, is:

\{\boldsymbol{u}+E\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda}:\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}\text{ solution for }\Psi\}=\!\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{\nu}~\text{substitution}\\
\text{considered in line~\ref{gcd-to-increasing:line-for-loop}}\end{subarray}}\!\{\boldsymbol{u}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}+E_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda}:\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{N}^{m-j}\text{ solution for }\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\},

(10)

where
j\geq 1
is the number of variables in
f
. The
procedure adds each triple
(\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\boldsymbol{u}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}},E_{\boldsymbol{\nu}})
to the set
B

(line
15
), so that it
will be tested for increasingness in a later iteration of
the
while
loop of
line
4
. Termination is
guaranteed from the fact that
f
is non-constant and so
each
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
has strictly fewer variables
than
\Psi
.

Lemma 13
.

Algorithm

3

respects its specification. On input
B
such that, for
every
(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)\in B
,
\Psi
has at most
d

variables and
k
GCD constraints, and
E
has at most

\ell
non-zero columns, each output triple
(\Psi^{\prime},\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime},E^{\prime})\in C
is such that
\Psi^{\prime}
has at most
d

variables and
k
GCD constraints,
E^{\prime}
has at most
\ell

non-zero columns,
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Psi^{\prime}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq 2^{15}(d+1)\cdot({|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+1)^{7}
,
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq(\ell+1)\cdot{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}^{2}
, and
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}E^{\prime}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}
.

Above,
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}
is the maximum among
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Psi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}
,

{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{u}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}
, and
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}E{|\mkern-2.0mu|}
, over all
gcd-to-div
triples
(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)\in B
. The most difficult parts
of the proof are the bounds on
\Psi^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}
. These,
however, follow from the properties of
gcd-to-div
triples
and, in particular, from the special shape of the
divisibility constraints that they allow.
Together,
Lemmas

10
and
13

imply
Proposition

1
in
Section

1.4
.

Proof.

The fact
that
Algorithm

3

respects its specification follows from the discussion
given above, and in particular
from
Lemma

11

and
Equation

10
. Let us then
focus on the bounds on an output triple
(\Psi^{\prime},\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime},E^{\prime})
. Note that
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\geq 1
, if
B
contains at
least one divisibility. Following the
while
loop
of
4
, there is a
sequence of triples

(\Psi_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{1},E_{1})\ \to\ (\Psi_{2},\boldsymbol{u}_{2},E_{2})\ \to\ \dots\ \to(\Psi_{k},\boldsymbol{u}_{k},E_{k})=(\Psi^{\prime},\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime},E^{\prime}),

where
(\Psi_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{1},E_{1})\in B
and for every
i\in[1,k-1]
, the triple
(\Psi_{i+1},\boldsymbol{u}_{i+1},E_{i+1})

is computed from
(\Psi_{i},\boldsymbol{u}_{i},E_{i})
following
lines
8
–
15
.
In particular, given
i\in[1,k-1]
:

•

there is a non-constant
polynomial
\widehat{f}_{i}
that is the part of a
left-hand side in
\Psi_{i}

satisfying
{\textup{M}_{\widehat{f}_{i}}(\Psi_{i})\cap\mathbb{Z}\neq\!\{0\}}
and with variables
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}\coloneqq(\lambda_{i,1},\dots,\lambda_{i,j_{i}})
, and

•

there is a map
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{i}:\{\lambda_{i,1},\dots,\lambda_{i,j_{i}}\}\to[0,\widehat{c}_{i}]
such that
\widehat{f}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{i}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}))
divides

\widehat{c}_{i}
, where
\widehat{c}_{i}
is the minimum
positive integer in
\textup{M}_{\widehat{f}_{i}}(\Psi_{i})
,

such that
\Psi_{i+1}=\Psi_{i}[\boldsymbol{\nu}_{i}(\lambda_{i,r})\mathbin{/}\lambda_{i,r}:r\in[1,j_{i}]]
,

\boldsymbol{u}_{i+1}=\boldsymbol{u}_{i}+\sum_{r=1}^{j}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{i}(\lambda_{i,r})\cdot\boldsymbol{p}_{r}
, where
\boldsymbol{p}_{r}
is
the column of
E_{i}
corresponding to the
variable
\lambda_{i,r}
, and
E_{i+1}
is obtained from

by removing the columns corresponding to variables
in
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}
. Note that this implies that

{|\mkern-2.0mu|}E^{\prime}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq{|\mkern-2.0mu|}E_{i}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}
and
that
E^{\prime}
and
E_{i}
have at most
\ell
non-zero columns,
as required by the lemma.

We show the remaining bounds in the statement of the lemma
by induction on
i\in[1,k]
, with the induction
hypothesis stating that
(\Psi_{i},\boldsymbol{u}_{i},E_{i})
is
a
gcd-to-div
triple where:

1.

\Psi_{i}
is a
system with at most
d
variables and
k

GCD constraints, having the form

\Psi_{i}=\bigwedge_{j=1}^{l}c_{j}\mathrel{|}f_{j}(\boldsymbol{y})\land\bigwedge_{j=l+1}^{n}\Big(z_{j}+c_{j}\mathrel{|}f_{j}(\boldsymbol{y})\land z_{j}+c_{j}\mathrel{|}g_{j}(\boldsymbol{y})\Big)\land\bigwedge_{j=n+1}^{m}\Big(f_{j}(\boldsymbol{y})\mathrel{|}w_{j}\land g_{j}(\boldsymbol{y})\mathrel{|}w_{j}+c_{j}\Big),

where
\boldsymbol{y}
,
\boldsymbol{z}=(z_{l+1},\dots,z_{n})
and
\boldsymbol{w}=(w_{n+1},\dots,w_{m})
are disjoint families of
variables (according to the definition of
gcd-to-div
triple),
c_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
for every
j\in[1,m]
, and

2.

for every
j\in[1,l]
,
c_{j}\leq 2^{15}\cdot(2+{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|})^{7}
, and
for every

,
c_{j}\leq{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}
, and

3.

for every
j\in[l+1,m]
,
h(\boldsymbol{y})\in\{f_{j}(\boldsymbol{y}),\,g_{j}(\boldsymbol{y})\}
has variable coefficients bounded
by
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}
, and constant bounded by
(d+1-d^{\prime})\cdot{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}^{2}
, where

is the number of
variables in

, and

4.

if
i\in[2,k]
,
then for every
r\in[1,j_{i-1}]
, if
\lambda_{i-1,r}

belongs to
\boldsymbol{y}
then
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{i}(\lambda_{i-1,r})\leq{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}
, and if
\lambda_{i-1,r}
belongs to

\boldsymbol{z}
then
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{i}(\lambda_{i-1,r})\leq 2^{14}(2+{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|})^{7}
.

Note that
Item

4
implies

{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq(\ell+1)\cdot{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}^{2}
,
since all non-zero columns of
E_{1}
correspond to
variables in
\boldsymbol{y}
, by definition of
gcd-to-div
triple.

Items

2
and
3

imply
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Psi^{\prime}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq 2^{15}(d+1)\cdot({|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+1)^{7}
.

base case
i=1
.

In this case
(\Psi_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{1},E_{1})\in B
and the hypothesis above trivially
holds since
(\Psi_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{1},E_{1})
is a
gcd-to-div
triple and
Properties
2
–
4

ensure that
\Psi_{1}
has the form
in
Item

1
.

induction step
i+1\geq 2
.

We assume the induction hypothesis for
(\Psi_{i},\boldsymbol{u}_{i},E_{i})
, and establish it for
(\Psi_{i+1},\boldsymbol{u}_{i+1},E_{i+1})
.
By
Lemma

12
,
(\Psi_{i+1},\boldsymbol{u}_{i+1},E_{i+1})
is a

triple,
hence

1
follows. So,
let us focus on establishing the part of the induction

\Psi_{i+1}
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{i}

(
Items

2
,
3
and
4
).
Let
\boldsymbol{z}
,
\boldsymbol{y}
and
\boldsymbol{w}
be the families of
variables witnessing that
(\Psi_{i},\boldsymbol{u}_{i},E_{i})
is a
gcd-to-div
triple, according to the
definition of such triples.
By Property
2
,

\widehat{f}_{i}
has variables from either
\boldsymbol{z}
or

\boldsymbol{y}
(not both). We divide the proof depending on
these two cases.

case
\widehat{f}_{i}
has only variables from
\boldsymbol{y}
.

From Property
2
of

gcd-to-div
triples,
\widehat{f}_{i}
only appears as
a left-hand side in divisibilities of the form
a\cdot\widehat{f}_{i}(\boldsymbol{y})\mathrel{|}h(\boldsymbol{w})
, with

a\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
. From
Property
4
of

gcd-to-div
triples together with the fact
that
\textup{M}_{\widehat{f}_{i}}(\Psi_{i})\cap\mathbb{Z}\neq\{0\}
, we conclude that there must be a
variable
w
in
\boldsymbol{w}
and

such
that
a_{1}\cdot\widehat{f}_{i}\mathrel{|}w
and
a_{2}\cdot\widehat{f}_{i}\mathrel{|}w+c
are divisibilities
in
\Psi_{i}
, for some
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
. Then,
c\in\textup{M}_{\widehat{f}_{i}}(\Psi_{i})
and by definition

\widehat{c}_{i}\leq c
. By induction hypothesis
(
Item

2
),
\widehat{c}_{i}\leq{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}
, which shows

Item

4
directly by
definition of
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{i}
.

Item

2
is also trivially
satisfied: since we are replacing only variables
in
\boldsymbol{y}
, all polynomials in
\Psi_{i+1}
with
variables from
\boldsymbol{z}
or
\boldsymbol{w}
are
polynomials in
\Psi_{i}
, and no new coefficient

c^{\prime}
can appear in divisibilities of the form
c^{\prime}\mathrel{|}f(\boldsymbol{y})
.

To prove
Item

3
, let

h^{\prime}
be a polynomial obtained from some
h(\boldsymbol{y})
in
\Psi_{i}
by evaluating each
\lambda_{i,r}

as
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{i}(\lambda_{i,r})
(
r\in[1,j]
). By
induction hypothesis
(
Item

3
),
h
has
variable coefficients bounded by
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}
,
and constants bounded by
(d+1-d^{\prime})\cdot{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}^{2}
,
where
d^{\prime}
is the number of variables in
h
.
Let
d^{\prime\prime}
be the number of variables in
h^{\prime}
.
Because of the substitutions done by
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{i}
,
we conclude that the coefficients of
h^{\prime}
are
bounded by
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}
, whereas its constant is
bounded by
(d+1-d^{\prime})\cdot{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}^{2}+(d^{\prime}-d^{\prime\prime})\cdot{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}^{2}=(d+1-d^{\prime\prime})\cdot{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}^{2}
.

case
\widehat{f}_{i}
has only variables from
\boldsymbol{z}
.

In this case,
\widehat{f}_{i}
is of the form
z+c
for some
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and by
Property
3
of
gcd-to-div
triple it appears in exactly two divisibilities
z+c\mathrel{|}f(\boldsymbol{y})
and
z+c\mathrel{|}g(\boldsymbol{y})
. In
order to upper bound
\widehat{c}_{i}
, we divide
the proof in two cases, depending on whether
(\mathbb{Z}f+\mathbb{Z}g)\cap\mathbb{Z}=\{0\}
.

case
(\mathbb{Z}f+\mathbb{Z}g)\cap\mathbb{Z}=\{0\}
.

Since
\textup{M}_{\widehat{f}_{i}}(\Psi_{i})\cap\mathbb{Z}\neq\{0\}
, by
Properties
2

and
4
of

gcd-to-div
triples there must be two
polynomials
f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})
and
g^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})
, a
variable
w
in
\boldsymbol{w}
and
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
such that
f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})\mathrel{|}w
,
g^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})\mathrel{|}w+c^{\prime}
and
\{a^{\prime}\cdot f^{\prime},\,b^{\prime}\cdot g^{\prime}\}\subseteq(\mathbb{Z}f+\mathbb{Z}g)
. Then, by
definition of divisibility module,
a^{\prime}\cdot b^{\prime}\cdot c^{\prime}\in\textup{M}_{\widehat{f}_{i}}(\Psi_{i})
. By induction
hypothesis
c^{\prime}\leq{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}

(
Item

2
), and
therefore to find a bound on
\widehat{c}_{i}

is suffices to bound
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
. Let us
study the case of
a^{\prime}
(the bound is the same
for
b^{\prime}
). The set
S\coloneqq\{-f^{\prime},f,g\}

can be represented as a matrix
A\in\mathbb{Z}^{(d+1)\times 3}
in which each column
contains the coefficients and the constant of
a distinct element of
S
. We
apply
Proposition

3
on the system
A\cdot(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=\boldsymbol{0}
, and conclude that

a^{\prime}
is bounded by
4\cdot(3\cdot\max(2,{|\mkern-2.0mu|}A{|\mkern-2.0mu|}))^{3}\leq 108\cdot({|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+1)^{3}
. Therefore,
\widehat{c}_{i}\leq 2^{14}({|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+1)^{7}
.

case
(\mathbb{Z}f+\mathbb{Z}g)\cap\mathbb{Z}\neq\{0\}
.

In this case, we consider the set
S\coloneqq\{f,g\}
and the matrix
A\in\mathbb{Z}^{(d+1)\times 2}
in which each column contains the
coefficients and the constant of a distinct
element in
S
, with the constant being places
in the last row. To find a non-zero value
c^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{Z}f+\mathbb{Z}g)\cap\mathbb{Z}
, we solve the
system
A\cdot(x_{1},x_{2})+x_{3}\cdot(\boldsymbol{0},1)=\boldsymbol{0}
. By
Proposition

3
,

\widehat{c}_{i}\leq|c^{\prime}|\leq 4\cdot(3\cdot\max(2,{|\mkern-2.0mu|}A{|\mkern-2.0mu|}))^{3}\leq 108\cdot({|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+1)^{3}
.

Therefore,
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{i}(z)\leq\widehat{c}_{i}\leq 2^{14}({|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+1)^{7}
, which
shows
Item

4
of the
induction hypothesis.

Item

3
is trivially
satisfied, since
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{i}
replaces only the
variable
z
, which does not belong to
\boldsymbol{y}
.

Item

2
follows from the
fact that in the polynomial
z+c
the integer

c
is bounded by
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}
by induction
hypothesis, and therefore
\boldsymbol{\nu}(z)+c\leq 2^{15}({|\mkern-2.0mu|}B{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+1)^{7}
.
∎

4.2
Bound on the solutions modulo primes

Through
Algorithms

2
and
3

we are able to compute from a IP-GCD system
\Phi
a set of

gcd-to-div
triples
C
such that
\{\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\boldsymbol{a}\text{ is a solution to }\Phi\}=\llbracket{C}\rrbracket
. To
apply
Theorem

4
, what is left is to
study bounds on the solutions modulo primes in

\mathbb{P}(\Psi)
, for every
(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)\in C
.

See
5

Proof.

Let us assume there exists a solution
\boldsymbol{\nu}\colon\boldsymbol{\lambda}\to\mathbb{Z}
to
\Psi(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
modulo
p
.
We build another solution
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}\colon\boldsymbol{\lambda}\to\mathbb{Z}
to
\Psi(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
modulo
p
such that

{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}){|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq(d+1)\cdot{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Psi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}^{3}p^{2}
. According to
Properties
2
–
4

of
gcd-to-div
triples, the formula
\Psi
is of the form:

\Psi=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{l}c_{i}\mathrel{|}f_{i}(\boldsymbol{y})\land\bigwedge_{i=l+1}^{n}\Big(z_{i}+c_{i}\mathrel{|}f_{i}(\boldsymbol{y})\land z_{i}+c_{i}\mathrel{|}g_{i}(\boldsymbol{y})\Big)\land\bigwedge_{i=n+1}^{m}\Big(f_{i}(\boldsymbol{y})\mathrel{|}w_{i}\land g_{i}(\boldsymbol{y})\mathrel{|}w_{i}+c_{i}\Big),

where
\boldsymbol{y}
,
\boldsymbol{z}=(z_{l+1},\dots,z_{n})
and
\boldsymbol{w}=(w_{n+1},\dots,w_{m})
are disjoint families of variables,
and
c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
for every
i\in[1,m]
. Recall that
the variables
z_{i}
(
i\in[l+1,n]
) are all distinct,
and the same holds true for the variables
w_{i}

(i\in[n+1,m]
). We define
\mu_{i}\coloneqq v_{p}(c_{i})
,
\mu\coloneqq\max_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{i}
, and
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}
as:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(x)\coloneqq\!\begin{cases}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\text{ modulo }p^{\mu})&\text{if }x\text{ belongs to }\boldsymbol{y},\\
1&\text{if }x=z_{i}\text{ for some }i\in[l+1,n]\text{ and }p\text{ divides }c_{i},\\
0&\text{if }x=z_{i}\text{ for some }i\in[l+1,n]\text{ and }p\text{ does not divide }c_{i},\\
p^{\mu+1}g_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}))\,{-}\,c_{i}&\text{if }x=w_{i}\text{ for some }i\,{\in}\,[n\,{+}\,1,m]\text{ and }p^{\mu_{i}+1}\,\text{does not divide}\,f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{y})),\\
p^{\mu+1}f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}))&\text{otherwise }(x=w_{i}\text{ for some }i\in[n+1,m]).\\
\end{cases}

Note that
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}
is defined recursively in the last
two cases; this recursion is on variables from
\boldsymbol{y}

and thus
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}
is well-defined. By definition,

p^{\mu+1}\leq{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Psi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\cdot p
, and therefore

{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(x){|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq(d+1)\cdot{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Psi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}^{3}p^{2}
for every variable
x
in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
. To conclude
the proof, let us show that
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}
is a solution for

\Psi
modulo
p
. The fact that
f(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))\neq 0
for every polynomial
f
in the
left-hand side of a divisibility in
\Psi
stems from

\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}
being defined to be non-negative for every
variable in
\boldsymbol{z}
and
\boldsymbol{y}
, and
f
having a
positive constant by Property
2

of
gcd-to-div
triples. So, we focus on showing that

v_{p}(f(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})))\leq v_{p}(g(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})))
for every divisibility
f\mathrel{|}g
in
\Psi
.

Let
i\in[1,l]
, and consider
c_{i}\mathrel{|}f_{i}(\boldsymbol{y})
. By
definition of
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}
,
{f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}))\equiv f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{y}))\pmod{p^{\mu+1}}}
, and therefore

v_{p}(f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})))=\min(\mu+1,v_{p}(f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{y}))))
. By definition of
\mu
, we have
c_{i}\not\equiv 0\pmod{p^{\mu+1}}
, i.e.,
v_{p}(c_{i})<\mu+1
.
We conclude that
v_{p}(c_{i})\leq v_{p}(f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})))
.

Let
i\in[l+1,n]
, and consider the divisibilities
z_{i}+c_{i}\mathrel{|}f_{i}(\boldsymbol{y})
and
z_{i}+c_{i}\mathrel{|}g_{i}(\boldsymbol{y})
.
By definition of
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}
we have
v_{p}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(z_{i})+c_{i})=0
, and so
v_{p}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(z_{i})+c_{i})\leq v_{p}(f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})))
and
v_{p}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(z_{i})+c_{i})\leq v_{p}(f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})))
.

Let
i\in[n+1,m]
. Assume first that
p^{\mu_{i}+1}
does
not divide
f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{y}))
, and so
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}
is
defined so that
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(w_{i})=p^{\mu+1}g_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}))-c_{i}
. The divisibility
g_{i}(\boldsymbol{y})\mathrel{|}w_{i}+c
is trivially satisfied by
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}
over the
integers, and thus also modulo
p
. By definition of
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}
we have
{f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}))\equiv f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{y}))\pmod{p^{\mu+1}}}
and therefore

p^{\mu_{i}+1}
does not divide
f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}))
. By
definition of
\mu_{i}
, this implies
{v_{p}(f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})))\leq v_{p}(c_{i})}
. From the definition of

\mu
,
v_{p}(p^{\mu+1}g_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})))>v_{p}(c_{i})

and therefore
v_{p}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(w_{i}))=v_{p}(c_{i})
, which
yield
v_{p}(f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})))\leq v_{p}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(w_{i}))
. Let us now assume that
p^{\mu_{i}+1}
divides

f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{y}))
, and so
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}
is defined so
that
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(w_{i})=p^{\mu+1}f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}))
.
Clearly, the divisibility
f_{i}(\boldsymbol{y})\mathrel{|}w_{i}
is
satisfied by
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}
over the integers, and thus also
modulo
p
. Since
\boldsymbol{\nu}
is a solution for
\Psi

modulo
p
, and
p^{\mu+1}
divides
f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{y}))
, we conclude that
p^{\mu+1}
divides
\boldsymbol{\nu}(w_{i})
. Then, by definition of
\mu
,
v_{p}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(w_{i}))>v_{p}(c_{i})
and therefore
v_{p}(g_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{y})))\leq v_{p}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(w_{i})+c_{i})=v_{p}(c_{i})
. By
definition of
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}
,
{g_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y}))\equiv g_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{y}))\pmod{p^{\mu+1}}}
and
{v_{p}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(w_{i})+c_{i})=v_{p}(c_{i})}
. We conclude that

{v_{p}(g_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})))\leq v_{p}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}(w_{i})+c_{i})}
.
∎

4.3
Proof
of
Theorem

1

Thanks
to
Lemmas

4
,
10
,
13
and
5
,
we obtain the part of
Theorem

1
not concerning
optimization as a corollary
of
Theorem

4
.

Corollary 1
.

Each feasible IP-GCD system has a solution of polynomial
bit length.

Let us now discuss the related integer programming
optimization problem. Consider an
IP-GCD
system

\Phi(\boldsymbol{x})
and the problem of minimizing (or maximizing)
a linear objective
\boldsymbol{c}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{x}
subject
to
\Phi(\boldsymbol{x})
. We
apply
Lemmas

10
and
13

on
\Phi(\boldsymbol{x})
to obtain a set
C
of
gcd-to-div
triples
only featuring
3
-increasing systems of divisibility constraints , and
with
\{\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\boldsymbol{a}\text{ solution to }\Phi\}=\llbracket{C}\rrbracket
. We
show the following characterization that implies the
optimization part of
Theorem

1
:

1.

if for every
(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)\in C
,
\Psi
is unsatisfiable over
\mathbb{N}
, then

\Phi
is unsatisfiable;

2.

else, if there is

(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)\in C
such that
\Psi
is satisfiable
over
\mathbb{N}
and the linear polynomial
{\boldsymbol{c}^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{u}+E\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda})}
has a variable in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
with strictly negative (resp. positive)
coefficient, then an optimal solution minimizing
(resp. maximizing)
\boldsymbol{c}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{x}
subject to

\Phi(\boldsymbol{x})
does not exist;

3.

else, an optimal solution
does exist, and in particular one with polynomial bit
length with respect to
\langle\Phi\rangle
and

\langle\boldsymbol{c}\rangle
.

Item

1
follows directly from the
equivalence
\{\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\boldsymbol{a}\text{ solution ot }\Phi\}=\llbracket{C}\rrbracket
. Let us focus on
Item

2
, which
we show for the case of minimization (the case of
maximization being analogous). Consider a triple
(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)\in C
such that
\Psi
is satisfiable and the linear
polynomial
f(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\coloneqq\boldsymbol{c}^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{u}+E\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda})
has a variable in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}

with strictly negative coefficient. Let
\boldsymbol{z}
,
\boldsymbol{y}

and
\boldsymbol{w}
be the disjoint families of variable witnessing
the fact that
(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)
is a
gcd-to-div
triple,
according to the definition of such triples.
By
Lemma

11
,
\Psi
is increasing for

\boldsymbol{z}\mathbin{\prec}\boldsymbol{y}\mathbin{\prec}\boldsymbol{w}
, and from
Property
5
of
gcd-to-div
triples,
all variables appearing in
f(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
with a non-zero
coefficient are from
\boldsymbol{y}
. Let
\widehat{y}
be a
variable appearing in
f
with a negative coefficient, and
consider an order
(\mathbin{\prec})\in(\boldsymbol{z}\mathbin{\prec}\boldsymbol{y}\mathbin{\prec}\boldsymbol{w})
for which
\widehat{y}
is the largest of
the variables appearing in
\boldsymbol{y}
. Since
\Psi
is
satisfiable over
\mathbb{N}
, it is satisfiable modulo every prime
in
\mathbb{P}(\Psi)
, and we can
apply
Algorithm

1
to compute a
solution
\boldsymbol{\nu}
over
\mathbb{N}
satisfying the property
highlighted in
Remark

1
: the
formula
\Psi[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x:x\mathbin{\prec}\widehat{y}\,]
has a solution for infinitely many positive
values of
\widehat{y}
. Since
\widehat{y}
is the largest
(for
\mathbin{\prec}
) variable appearing in
f
, and its
coefficient in
f
is negative, we conclude that
\min\{f(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\in\mathbb{Z}:\boldsymbol{\lambda}\text{ is a solution
to }\Psi\}
is undefined, which in turn implies that an
optimal solution minimizing
\boldsymbol{c}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{x}

subject to
\Phi(\boldsymbol{x})
does not exist.

Lastly, let us consider
Item

3
. Again we
focus on the case of minimization. Below, let
C^{\prime}\coloneqq\{(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)\in C:\Psi\text{ is satisfiable over
}\mathbb{N}\}
and note that
\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\Phi(\boldsymbol{x})\}=\llbracket{C^{\prime}}\rrbracket
.
As
Items

1
and
2
do not hold,

C^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
and every
gcd-to-div
triple
(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)\in C^{\prime}
is such that the linear polynomial
\boldsymbol{c}^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{u}+E\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda})
only has
non-negative coefficients. Since the variables
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
are interpreted over
\mathbb{N}
, this means that
\ell\coloneqq\min\{\boldsymbol{c}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{u}:(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)\in C^{\prime}\}
is a lower bound to the values that
\boldsymbol{c}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{x}
can take when
\boldsymbol{x}
is a solution
to
\Phi
; i.e., the optimal solution exists.

Lemmas

10
and
13

ensure that the lower bound
\ell
has polynomial bit length
with respect to
\langle\Phi\rangle
and
\langle\boldsymbol{c}\rangle
.
We also have an upper bound
u
to the optimal solution: it
suffices to take the minimum of the values
(\boldsymbol{u}+E\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda})
, where
(\Psi,\boldsymbol{u},E)\in C^{\prime}
and

\boldsymbol{\lambda}
is the positive integer solution to
\Psi

computed with
Algorithm

1
using the
solutions modulo
p\in\mathbb{P}(\Psi)

of
Lemma

5
. Again,
u

has polynomial bit length with respect to
\langle\Phi\rangle

and
\langle\boldsymbol{c}\rangle
, thanks
to
Lemmas

4
,
10
and
13
,
and
Theorem

4
.

Item

3
then follows by reduction from the
feasibility problem of IP-GCD systems: it suffices to find
the minimal
v\in[\ell,u]
such that the IP-GCD system

\Phi_{v}(\boldsymbol{x})\coloneqq\Phi(\boldsymbol{x})\land(\boldsymbol{c}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{x}\leq v)
is feasible. Since every
v\in[\ell,u]
is of polynomial bit length,
by
Corollary

1
if
\Phi_{v}(\boldsymbol{x})

is satisfiable, then it has a solution
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}

such that
\langle\boldsymbol{x}\rangle\leq\mathrm{poly}(\langle\Phi\rangle,\langle\boldsymbol{c}\rangle)
.

Appendix A
Lemma

1
: proof of
1

In this appendix, we present the technical manipulation yielding
1
, hence finishing the proof of
Lemma

1
. Below,
\mu
and
\omega
stand for the Möbius function and the prime omega function, respectively. Recall that
\mu(n)=(-1)^{\omega(n)}
and
\omega(n)=\#\mathbb{P}(n)
, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.

Proposition 4

(Möbius inversion
[
7
, Theorem 266]
)
.

Consider two functions
f,g\colon\mathbb{Z}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
f(n)=\sum_{d\in\textup{div}(n)}g(d)
. For every
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

g(m)=\sum_{d\in\textup{div}(m)}f(d)\cdot\mu(\frac{m}{d})
.

Proposition 5

(Möbius sums
[
7
, Theorem 263]
)
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
greater than
1
,
\sum_{s\in\textup{div}(n)}\mu(s)=0
.

The following lemma tells us what to expect when we truncate the sum of the previous proposition so that it only considers elements with at most
\ell
divisors.

Lemma 14
.

Let
n,\ell\,{\in}\,\mathbb{N}
with
n
square-free. If
\omega(n)>\ell
then
\sum_{r\in\textup{div}(n),\,\omega(r)\leq\ell}\,\mu(r)=(-1)^{\ell}{\omega(n)-1\choose\ell}
.

Proof.

We write LHS (resp. RHS) for the left-hand (resp. right-hand) side of the equivalence in the statement.
Note that
\omega(n)>\ell
implies
n\geq 1
.
The proof is by induction on
\ell
.

Base case:
\ell=0
:

In this case,

\text{LHS}=\mu(1)=1=(-1)^{0}{\omega(n)-1\choose 0}=\text{RHS}
.

Induction step:
\ell\geq 1
:

We have,

LHS

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\in\textup{div}(n),\,\omega(r)<\ell}\mu(r)+\sum_{s\in\textup{div}(n),\,\omega(r)=\ell}\mu(s)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell-1}{\omega(n)-1\choose\ell-1}+\sum_{s\in\textup{div}(n),\,\omega(r)=\ell}\mu(s)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{by induction hypothesis;}\\
\text{recall $\omega(n)>\ell$}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell-1}\left({\omega(n)-1\choose\ell-1}-\sum_{r\in\textup{div}(n),\,\omega(r)=\ell}1\right)

since
\mu(r)=(-1)^{\ell}
iff
\omega(r)=\ell

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell-1}\left({\omega(n)-1\choose\ell-1}-{\omega(n)\choose\ell}\right)

from
n
square-free

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell}{\omega(n)-1\choose\ell}=\text{RHS}

Pascal’s rule.

We are now ready to prove
1
:

See
1

Let us recall the hypothesis under which this claim must be proved:
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
is odd,
d\geq 1
,
Q
is a non-empty finite set of primes,
Q(\ell)\coloneqq\{r\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q):\omega(r)\leq\ell\}
,

m
is a multiplicative function such that
m(q)\leq q-1
and
m(q)\leq d
on all
q\in Q
, and
W_{m}(Q)\coloneqq\prod_{q\in Q}\left(1-\frac{m(q)}{q}\right)
.

Proof.

We start by defining the truncated Möbius function
\mu_{\ell}
and its companion function
\psi_{\ell}
:
