\mu_{\ell}(x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mu(x)&\text{if $\omega(x)\leq\ell$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\psi_{\ell}(x)\coloneqq\sum_{r\in\textup{div}(x)}\mu_{\ell}(x).

The proof proceeds by performing two term manipulations.
In the first one, we use the fact that
m
is multiplicative,
together with properties of the Möbius function (e.g.
Proposition

4
), to show that

\sum_{r\in Q(\ell)}\frac{\mu(r)\cdot m(r)}{r}=W_{m}(Q)\cdot\left(1+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)\\
\omega(s)>\ell\end{subarray}}\frac{\psi_{\ell}(s)\cdot m(s)}{s\cdot W_{m}(\mathbb{P}(s))}\right).

(11)

In the second manipulation, we look at the sum
\sum_{s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)\setminus\{1\}}\frac{\psi_{\ell}(s)\cdot m(s)}{s\cdot W_{m}(\mathbb{P}(s))}
from the equation above, and (also thanks to
Lemma

14
) bound it in absolute terms as follows:

\left|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)\\
\omega(s)>\ell\end{subarray}}\frac{\psi_{\ell}(s)\cdot m(s)}{s\cdot W_{m}(\mathbb{P}(s))}\right|\leq\left(\frac{e\cdot\alpha}{\ell}\right)^{\ell}\cdot\alpha\cdot e^{\alpha},\ \text{ where }\alpha\coloneqq(d+1)^{2}(2+\ln\ln(\#Q+1)).

(12)

1

follows directly from
Equation

11
and
Equation

12
. Note that these equations can be used to also establish the upper bound to
\sum_{r\in Q(\ell)}\frac{\mu(r)\cdot m(r)}{r}
required for the upper bound of
Lemma

1
.

Manipulation resulting in
Equation

11
:

\displaystyle\ \ \sum_{r\in Q(\ell)}\frac{\mu(r)\cdot m(r)}{r}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\sum_{r\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)}\frac{\mu_{\ell}(r)\cdot m(r)}{r}

by def. of
\mu_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\sum_{r\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)}\frac{\left(\sum_{s\in\textup{div}(r)}\psi_{\ell}(s)\cdot\mu\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)\right)\cdot m(r)}{r}

by
Proposition

4

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\sum_{r\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)}\sum_{s\in\textup{div}(r)}\frac{\psi_{\ell}(s)\cdot\mu\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)\cdot m(r)}{r}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)}\sum_{r\in\textup{div}\left(\frac{\Pi Q}{s}\right)}\frac{\psi_{\ell}(s)\cdot\mu(r)\cdot m(r\cdot s)}{r\cdot s}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{invert summations using the}\\
\text{change of variable $r\leftarrow r\cdot s$}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)}\frac{\psi_{\ell}(s)\cdot m(s)}{s}\cdot\sum_{r\in\textup{div}\left(\frac{\Pi Q}{s}\right)}\frac{\mu(r)\cdot m(r)}{r}

multiplicity of
m

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)}\frac{\psi_{\ell}(s)\cdot m(s)}{s}\cdot\prod_{q\in Q\setminus\textup{div}(s)}\left(1+\frac{\mu(q)\cdot m(q)}{q}\right)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{multiplicity of~$\mu$ and~$m$;}\\
\text{factorization thanks to $r$ being}\\
\text{square-free, for all $r\in\textup{div}\!\textstyle\left(\frac{\Pi Q}{s}\right)$}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)}\frac{\psi_{\ell}(s)\cdot m(s)}{s}\cdot\frac{\prod_{q\in Q}\left(1-\frac{m(q)}{q}\right)}{\prod_{q\in\mathbb{P}(s)}\left(1-\frac{m(q)}{q}\right)}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{$\mu(q)=-1$ for $q$ prime}\\
\text{and simple manipulation}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)}\frac{\psi_{\ell}(s)\cdot m(s)}{s}\cdot\frac{W_{m}(Q)}{W_{m}(\mathbb{P}(s))}

by def. of
W_{m}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\,W_{m}(Q)\cdot\sum_{s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)}\frac{\psi_{\ell}(s)\cdot m(s)}{s\cdot W_{m}(\mathbb{P}(s))}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\,W_{m}(Q)\cdot\left(\sum_{s\in Q(\ell)}\frac{\psi_{\ell}(s)\cdot m(s)}{s\cdot W_{m}(\mathbb{P}(s))}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)\\
\omega(s)>\ell\end{subarray}}\frac{\psi_{\ell}(s)\cdot m(s)}{s\cdot W_{m}(\mathbb{P}(s))}\right)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{split depending on $\omega(s)\leq\ell$,}\\
\text{and by def.~of~$Q(\ell)$}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\,W_{m}(Q)\cdot\left(\sum_{s\in Q(\ell)}\frac{\left(\sum_{r\in\textup{div}(s)}\mu(r)\right)\cdot m(s)}{s\cdot W_{m}(\mathbb{P}(s))}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)\\
\omega(s)>\ell\end{subarray}}\frac{\psi_{\ell}(s)\cdot m(s)}{s\cdot W_{m}(\mathbb{P}(s))}\right)\hskip-39.83368pt

def. of
\psi_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\,W_{m}(Q)\cdot\left(1+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)\\
\omega(s)>\ell\end{subarray}}\frac{\psi_{\ell}(s)\cdot m(s)}{s\cdot W_{m}(\mathbb{P}(s))}\right)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{in the left summation:}\\
\text{for $s=1$ the addend is $1$,}\\
\text{and for $s>1$ the addend is $0$}\\
\text{by~\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:Mobius-sums}.}\end{aligned}

Manipulation resulting in
Equation

12
:

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)\\
\omega(s)>\ell\end{subarray}}\frac{\psi_{\ell}(s)\cdot m(s)}{s\cdot W_{m}(\mathbb{P}(s))}\right|\leq\,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)\\
\omega(s)>\ell\end{subarray}}{\omega(s)-1\choose\ell}\cdot\frac{m(s)}{s\cdot W_{m}(\mathbb{P}(s))}

by
Lemma

14
and def. of
\psi_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=\ell+1}^{\#Q}\left(\binom{k-1}{\ell}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)\\
\omega(s)=k\end{subarray}}\frac{m(s)}{s\cdot W_{m}(\mathbb{P}(s))}\right)

split on the value of
\omega(s)
.

We focus on the summation
\sum_{s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q),\,\omega(s)=k}\frac{m(s)}{s\cdot W_{m}(\mathbb{P}(s))}
.
Since the function
m
is multiplicative, and similarly
W_{m}(A\cup B)=W_{m}(A)\cdot W_{m}(B)
for
A,B
disjoint finite sets of primes (and
W_{m}(\emptyset)=1
by definition), for
k\geq 1
we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)\\
\omega(s)=k\end{subarray}}\frac{m(s)}{s\cdot W_{m}(\mathbb{P}(s))}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q_{1}<...<q_{k}\in Q}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{m(q_{i})}{q_{i}\cdot W_{m}(\{q_{i}\})}\right)\leq\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{q_{1},...,q_{k}\in Q}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{m(q_{i})}{q_{i}\cdot W_{m}(\{q_{i}\})}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\left(\sum_{q\in Q}\frac{m(q)}{q\cdot W_{m}(\{q\})}\right)^{k}=\frac{1}{k!}\left(\sum_{q\in Q}\frac{m(q)}{q-m(q)}\right)^{k}.

We further analyse the summation
\sum_{q\in Q}\frac{m(q)}{q-m(q)}
. Below, we write
Q_{d+1}
for the set of the first
\min(\#Q,d+1)
many primes in
Q
(recall
d\geq 1
), and denote by
p_{i}
the
i
-th prime.

\displaystyle\sum_{q\in Q}\frac{m(q)}{q-m(q)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q\in Q_{d+1}}\frac{m(q)}{q-m(q)}+\sum_{q\in Q\setminus Q_{d+1}}\frac{m(q)}{q-m(q)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{q\in Q_{d+1}}d+\sum_{q\in Q\setminus Q_{d+1}}\frac{m(q)}{q-m(q)}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{since }m(q)\leq d\\
\text{and }q-m(q)\geq 1\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot(d+1)+\sum_{q\in Q\setminus Q_{d+1}}\frac{d}{q-d}

m(q)\leq d<q
, for all
q\in Q\setminus Q_{d+1}

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot(d+1)+\sum_{i=d+2}^{\#Q}\frac{d}{p_{i}-d}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{def.~of~$Q\setminus Q_{d+1}$}\\
\text{and $p_{i}>d$ for $i\geq d+2$}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot(d+1)+d\cdot\sum_{i=d+2}^{\#Q}\frac{1}{(i\ln i)-d}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} p_{i}\geq i\ln i\text{ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Rosser39}{}{}]}}\\
\text{and $i\ln i>d$ for $i\geq d+2$}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot(d+1)+d\cdot(d+1)\sum_{i=d+2}^{\#Q}\frac{1}{i\ln i}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{since }\ \frac{1}{x\ln x-y}\leq\frac{y+1}{x\ln x}\\
\text{for all }x\geq 3\text{ and }0\leq y\leq x-1\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot(d+1)\cdot\Big(1+\sum_{i=3}^{\#Q}\frac{1}{i\ln i}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot(d+1)\cdot\Big(1+\int_{2}^{\#Q+1}\frac{1}{x\ln x}\textup{d}x\Big)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{Riemann over-approximation}\\
\text{note: $\#Q+1\geq 2$}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot(d+1)\cdot(1+\ln\ln(\#Q+1)-\ln\ln 2)

\displaystyle\leq(d+1)^{2}(2+\ln\ln(\#Q+1))\ =\ \alpha.

We combine this bound with the previous two to obtain complete the proof of
Equation

12
:

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\textup{div}(\Pi Q)\\
\omega(s)>\ell\end{subarray}}\frac{\psi_{\ell}(s)\cdot m(s)}{s\cdot W_{m}(\mathbb{P}(s))}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=\ell+1}^{\#Q}\left(\binom{k-1}{\ell}\cdot\frac{1}{k!}\cdot\alpha^{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\#Q-\ell-1}\left(\binom{\ell+j}{\ell}\cdot\frac{1}{(\ell+1+j)!}\cdot\alpha^{\ell+1+j}\right)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{change of variable}\\
k\leftarrow\ell+1+j\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\#Q-\ell-1}\left(\frac{(\ell+j)!}{\ell!\cdot j!\cdot(\ell+1+j)!}\cdot\alpha^{\ell+1+j}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha^{\ell+1}}{\ell!}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^{j}}{j!}

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\begin{aligned} \text{note: all terms in the}\\
\text{summation are non-negative}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha^{\ell+1}}{\ell!}\cdot e^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{def.~of $e^{x}$ as a series}\\
\text{i.e.,~$\textstyle e^{x}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{x^{i}}{i!}$}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{e\cdot\alpha}{\ell}\right)^{\ell}\cdot\alpha\cdot e^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\text{from }x!\geq\frac{x^{x}}{e^{x}}.

This completes the proof
of
1
.
∎

Appendix B
Theorem

3
: proofs of
2
and
3

The mathematical objects appearing in the statements of the two claims below are defined in the proof of
Theorem

3
and the statement of
Lemma

1
; see
Section

2
.

See
2

Proof.

Recall that
A=[k,k+z]\cap S_{M}
, and so
A\cap S_{\alpha,r}=[k,k+z]\cap S_{M}\cap S_{\alpha,r}
. Since that elements in
M\cup Q

are pairwise coprime and
M\cap Q=\emptyset
,
we can apply the CRT and conclude that

S_{M}\cap S_{\alpha,r}
is an
arithmetic progression with period
r\cdot\Pi M
. Let

u
be the largest element of
S_{M}\cap S_{\alpha,r}

that is strictly smaller than
k
. By definition of
u

and from the fact that
S_{M}\cap S_{\alpha,r}
has period

r\cdot\Pi M
, we get
\#(A\cap S_{\alpha,r})=\left\lfloor\frac{k+z-u}{r\cdot\Pi M}\right\rfloor
. Similarly, because

S_{M}
is periodic in
\Pi M
,
\left\lfloor\frac{k+z-u}{\Pi M}\right\rfloor
is over counting
\#A
by at most
r-1
, i.e.,
there is
\tau_{\alpha,r}\in[0,r-1]
such that
\#A=\left\lfloor\frac{k+z-u}{\Pi M}\right\rfloor-\tau_{\alpha,r}
. Since

\left\lfloor\frac{a}{b}\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor\frac{\left\lfloor a\right\rfloor}{b}\right\rfloor
for
every
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, we get
\#(A\cap S_{\alpha,r})=\left\lfloor\frac{1}{r}\cdot(\#A+\tau_{\alpha,r})\right\rfloor
. With a simple manipulation using

\left\lfloor a\right\rfloor+\left\lfloor b\right\rfloor\leq\left\lfloor a+b\right\rfloor\leq\left\lfloor a\right\rfloor+\left\lfloor b\right\rfloor+1
and
\left\lfloor\frac{\tau_{\alpha,r}}{r}\right\rfloor=0
,
we derive
\frac{\#A}{r}-1\leq\#(A\cap S_{\alpha,r})\leq\frac{\#A}{r}+1
.
∎

See
3

Proof.

Let
Q_{d}
be the set containing the
\min(\#Q,d)
smallest primes in
Q
.
Recall
that by definition
m(q)\leq d\leq q-1
for every
q\in Q
. We have,

W_{m}(Q)^{-1}=\prod_{q\in Q}\frac{q}{q-m(q)}\ \leq\prod_{q\in Q}\frac{q}{q-d}\ \leq\ \prod_{q\in Q_{d}}\frac{q}{q-d}\cdot\!\prod_{q\in Q\setminus Q_{d}}\frac{q}{q-d}\ \leq\ (d+1)^{d}\cdot\!\prod_{q\in Q\setminus Q_{d}}\frac{q}{q-d},

where the last inequality holds because
\frac{x}{x-c}\leq c+1
for every
x\geq c+1
and
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
Below, let us denote by
p_{i}
the
i
-th prime.
We further inspect the product
\prod_{q\in Q\setminus Q_{d}}\frac{q}{q-d}
:

\displaystyle\prod_{q\in Q\setminus Q_{d}}\frac{q}{q-d}\leq\prod_{i=d+1}^{\#Q}\frac{p_{i}}{p_{i}-d}\leq\prod_{i=d+1}^{\#Q}\frac{i\cdot\ln i}{i\cdot\ln i-d}

\displaystyle\hskip-142.26378pt\begin{aligned} \text{$p_{i}\geq i\cdot\ln i$ for all $i\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}$, see~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Rosser39}{}{}]};}\\
\text{$x\mapsto\frac{x}{x-d}$ decreasing for $x>1$}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\exp\left({\sum_{i=d+1}^{\#Q}\ln\Big(\frac{i\cdot\ln i}{i\cdot\ln i-d}\Big)}\right)=\exp\left({-\sum_{i=d+1}^{\#Q}\ln\Big(1-\frac{d}{i\cdot\ln i}\Big)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\exp\left({\sum_{i=d+1}^{\#Q}\frac{3\cdot d}{i\cdot\ln i}}\right)\leq\exp\left({\sum_{i=2}^{\#Q}\frac{3\cdot d}{i\cdot\ln i}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-284.52756pt\begin{aligned} \text{first term from $\ln\Big(1-\frac{1}{x}\Big)\geq-\frac{3}{x}$ for all $x\geq\ln 3$;}\\
\text{for corner case $d=1$ and $i=2$, note $2\ln 2>\ln 3$}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{3\cdot d}{2\cdot\ln 2}+{\sum_{i=3}^{\#Q}\frac{3\cdot d}{i\cdot\ln i}}\right)\leq\exp\left(\frac{3\cdot d}{2\cdot\ln 2}+\int_{2}^{\#Q+1}\frac{3\cdot d}{x\ln x}\textup{d}x\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-142.26378pt\begin{aligned} \text{Riemann over-approximation}\\
\text{note: $\#Q+1\geq 2$}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{3\cdot d}{2\cdot\ln 2}+3\cdot d\cdot\big(\ln\ln(\#Q+1)-\ln\ln 2\,\big)\right)\leq\exp\left(3\cdot d\cdot\big(2+\ln\ln(\#Q+1)\big)\right).

We plug this bound on the afore-derived
bound for
W_{m}(Q)^{-1}
to complete the proof
of
3
:

\displaystyle W_{m}(Q)^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq\,(d+1)^{d}\exp\left(3\cdot d\cdot\big(2+\ln\ln(\#Q+1)\big)\right)\,\leq\,(d+1)^{d}\cdot e^{6\cdot d}\ln(\#Q+1)^{3\cdot d}

\displaystyle\leq\,(d+1)^{d}\cdot 2^{9\cdot d}\ln(\#Q+1)^{3\cdot d}\,\leq\,(d+1)^{10\cdot d}\ln(\#Q+1)^{3\cdot d}.\qed

Appendix C
Algorithms related to the elimination property

In this appendix we establish
Lemma

6
and
Lemma

7
.
Proving these lemmas require the standard notion of kernel and Hermite normal form of a matrix, which we now recall for completeness.
Consider a matrix
{A\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times d}}
.
The
kernel
of
A
is the vector space
\ker(A)\coloneqq\{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:A\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{0}\}
.
We represent
bases
of
\ker(A)
as matrices
K\in\mathbb{Z}^{d\times(d-r)}
, where
r
is the rank of
A
and
\ker(A)=\{K\cdot\boldsymbol{v}:\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-r}\}
.
A matrix
H\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times d}
is said to be the
column-style Hermite normal form
of
A
(
HNF
, in short) if there is a square unimodular matrix
U\in\mathbb{Z}^{d\times d}
such that
H=A\cdot U
and

1.

H
is lower triangular,

2.

the
pivot
(i.e., the first non-zero entry in a column, from the top) of a non-zero column is positive and it is strictly below the pivot of the column before it, and

3.

elements to the right of pivots are
0
and elements to the left are non-negative and smaller than the pivot.

Recall that
U
being unimodular means that it is invertible over the integers.

Given a vector
\boldsymbol{v}
, we write
\boldsymbol{v}[i]

for the
i
-th entry of
\boldsymbol{v}
, starting at
i=1
.
Similarly, for a matrix
A
, we write
A[i]
for its
i
-th row, again starting at
i=1
.

Proposition 6

(
[
19
, Section 4.2]
)
.

The HNF
H
of a matrix
A\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times d}
always exits, it is unique, and
A
and
H
generate the same lattice, i.e.,
\{A\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda}:\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\}=\{H\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda}:\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\}
.

The following proposition refers to the LLL-based algorithm for the HNF in
[
8
]
. A basis for the integer kernel can be retrieved from the HNF together with the associated unimodular matrix.

Proposition 7

(
[
24
]
)
.

There is a
PTIME
algorithm computing a basis
K

of the integer kernel and the HNF
H

of an input matrix
A\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times d}
.
The algorithm yields
{{|\mkern-2.0mu|}K{|\mkern-2.0mu|},{|\mkern-2.0mu|}H{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq(n\cdot{|\mkern-2.0mu|}A{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+1)^{O(n)}}
.

Note that we can also upper bound the GCDs of the rows of the integer kernel
K
in terms of the rank of
A
by appealing to
Proposition

3
.

Corollary 2
.

Consider a basis
K
of the integer kernel of a matrix
A\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times d}
. Let
r\coloneqq\textup{rank}(A)
.
For every
i\in[1,d]
,
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\gcd(K[i]){|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq(d+1)\cdot(r\cdot\max(2,{|\mkern-2.0mu|}A{|\mkern-2.0mu|}))^{r}
.

C.1
Computing a set spanning the divisibility module

See
6

This lemma follows from the forthcoming
Proposition

8
and
Proposition

9
.

For the whole section, let
\Phi\coloneqq\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}\mathrel{|}g_{i}
and
f
be a primitive polynomial.
As already explained in
Section

3
,
the algorithm
Lemma

6
refers to performs a fix-point
computation where, at the
\ell
-th iteration, the values contained in
\boldsymbol{v}
characterize a spanning set of a particular submodule
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
of
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)
.
More precisely, we define
\textup{M}^{0}_{f}(\Phi)\subseteq\textup{M}^{1}_{f}(\Phi)\subseteq\dots\subseteq\textup{M}^{\ell}_{f}(\Phi)\subseteq\dots
to be the sequence of sets given by

1.

\textup{M}_{f}^{0}(\Phi)\coloneqq\mathbb{Z}f
, and

2.

for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell+1}(\Phi)\coloneqq\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)+\left\{\textstyle\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}\cdot g_{j}:\text{ for all }i\in[1,m],\,a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ and }a_{i}\cdot f_{i}\in\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)\right\}\!.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.
Note that, by definition,
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-module and moreover if
\mathbb{Z}f_{i}\cap\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)=\{0\}
for some
i\in[1,m]
, then
a_{i}
in the definition of
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell+1}(\Phi)
equals
0
.
We define the
canonical representation
of
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
as the vector
(v_{1},\dots,v_{m})\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
such that for every
i\in[1,m]
,

•

if
\ell=0
then
v_{i}\coloneqq 0
,

•

if
\ell\geq 1
then
v_{i}\coloneqq\gcd\{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}:\lambda\cdot f_{i}\in\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell-1}(\Phi)\}
.

Lemma

16
shows that this vector represents a spanning set of
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
, but first we need an auxiliary lemma.

Lemma 15
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
(v_{1},\dots,v_{m})
and

(v_{1}^{\prime},\dots,v_{m}^{\prime})
be the canonical
representations of
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
and

\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell+1}(\Phi)
, respectively. For every
i\in[1,m]
,
v_{i}=v_{i}^{\prime}=0
or
v_{i}^{\prime}
divides

v_{i}
(so,
v_{i}^{\prime}\neq 0
if
v_{i}\neq 0
).

Proof.

Let
i\in[1,m]
. If
v_{i}=0
then either
v_{i}^{\prime}
is

0
or it divides
v_{i}
, hence the statement is
trivially satisfied for that particular
i
.
Suppose that
v_{i}\neq 0
. By definition of canonical
representation,
\ell\geq 1
and
v_{i}\cdot f_{i}\in M^{\ell-1}_{f}(\Phi)
. By definition of
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
,
we conclude that
v_{i}\cdot f_{i}\in M^{\ell}_{f}(\Phi)
. By definition of canonical
representation
v_{i}^{\prime}=\gcd\{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}:\lambda\cdot f_{i}\in\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)\}
, and
therefore
v_{i}^{\prime}
divides
v_{i}
.
∎

Lemma 16
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and let
(v_{1},\dots,v_{m})\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
be the canonical representation of

\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
. Then, the set of linear
polynomials
\{f,\,v_{1}\cdot g_{1},\,\dots,\,v_{m}\cdot g_{m}\}

spans
M^{\ell}_{f}(\Phi)
.

Proof.

The statement follows by induction on
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

base case
\ell=0
.

From
\textup{M}_{f}^{0}(\Phi)=\mathbb{Z}f
we have

(v_{1},\dots,v_{m})=(0,\dots,0)
and
\{f\}
spans

M^{0}_{f}(\Phi)
.

induction step
\ell\geq 1
.

From the induction
hypothesis,
{\{f,\,v_{1}^{*}\cdot g_{1},\,\dots,\,v_{m}^{*}\cdot g_{m}\}}

spans
M^{\ell-1}_{f}(\Phi)
; with
(v_{1}^{*},\dots,v_{m}^{*})
being the canonical representation
of
M^{\ell-1}_{f}(\Phi)
.
We consider the two inclusions of the equivalence

\mathbb{Z}f+\mathbb{Z}(v_{1}\cdot g_{1})+\dots+\mathbb{Z}(v_{m}\cdot g_{m})=M^{\ell}_{f}(\Phi)
.

(\subseteq):
This direction follows directly by definition of
M^{\ell}_{f}(\Phi)
.

(\supseteq):
Let
h\in\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
.
By definition,

h=h_{1}+h_{2}
where
{h_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}f+\mathbb{Z}(v_{1}^{*}\cdot g_{1})+\dots+\mathbb{Z}(v_{m}^{*}\cdot g_{m})}

and
h_{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\cdot g_{i}\in\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
satisfying
{a_{i}\cdot f_{i}\in\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell-1}(\Phi)}
for every
i\in[1,m]
.
By
Lemma

15

\mathbb{Z}(v_{i}^{*}\cdot g_{i})\subseteq\mathbb{Z}(v_{i}\cdot g_{i})
and therefore
h_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}f+\mathbb{Z}(v_{1}\cdot g_{1})+\dots+\mathbb{Z}(v_{m}\cdot g_{m})
.
By definition
v_{i}=\gcd\{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}:\lambda\cdot f_{i}\in\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell-1}(\Phi)\}
and thus
v_{i}\mathrel{|}a_{i}
.
So,
h\in\mathbb{Z}f+\mathbb{Z}(v_{1}\cdot g_{1})+\dots+\mathbb{Z}(v_{m}\cdot g_{m})
.
∎

Lemma 17
.

(A) For
every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}\subseteq\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell+1}\subseteq\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)
.

(B) There is
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)=\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell+1}(\Phi)
.

(C) For
every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, if
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)=\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell+1}(\Phi)
then
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)=\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)
.

Proof.

Proof of
(
17
): By definition,

\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}\subseteq\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell+1}
.
An
induction on
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
shows
M^{\ell}_{f}(\Phi)\subseteq\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)
:

base case
\ell=0
:

By definition of
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
and of divisibility module,
M^{0}_{f}(\Phi)=\mathbb{Z}f\subseteq\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)
.

induction case
\ell\geq 1
:

From the induction hypothesis,

M^{\ell-1}_{f}(\Phi)\subseteq\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)
.
By definition,

M^{\ell}_{f}(\Phi)
is defined from

M^{\ell-1}_{f}(\Phi)
by taking linear
combinations of elements in
M^{\ell-1}_{f}(\Phi)

together with elements
b\cdot h
such that
b\cdot g\in M^{\ell-1}_{f}(\Phi)
and
g\mathrel{|}h
is
a divisibility of
\Phi
. From the definition of
divisibility module,
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)
is closed under
such combinations, since for every
b\cdot g\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)
and
g\mathrel{|}h
divisibility of
\Phi
,

b\cdot h\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)
(see
Property
3
in the def. of
divisibility module). From
M^{\ell-1}_{f}(\Phi)\subseteq\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)
we then conclude that

M^{\ell}_{f}(\Phi)\subseteq\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)
.

Proof of
(
17
): This statement
follows from
Lemma

15
. Indeed, for a given

\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the canonical
representations
(v_{1},\dots,v_{m})
and

(v_{1}^{\prime},\dots,v_{m}^{\prime})
of
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
and

\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell+1}(\Phi)
, respectively.
By
Lemma

15
, if
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)\neq\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell+1}(\Phi)
then one of the following holds:

1.

there is
i\in[1,m]
such
that
v_{i}=0
and
v_{i}^{\prime}\neq 0
, or

2.

there is
i\in[1,m]
such
that
v_{i}\neq 0
,
v_{i}^{\prime}\neq v_{i}
and
v_{i}^{\prime}

divides
v_{i}
.

Again from
Lemma

15
, for
every
j\in[1,m]
, if
v_{j}\neq 0
then
v_{j}^{\prime}

divides
v_{j}
. This implies that both
Items (
1
) and (
2
) cannot
occur infinitely often, and therefore
\textup{M}_{f}^{r}(\Phi)=\textup{M}_{f}^{r+1}(\Phi)
for some
r\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof of
(
17
):
From Part (
17
),
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)\subseteq\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)
.
We show that
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
satisfies the Properties
1
–
3

of divisibility modules. Then,
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)\subseteq\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)

follows from the minimality condition required by these modules.
Properties
1

and
2
are trivially satisfied. To establish
Property
3
, consider
b\cdot g\in\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
and a divisibility
g\mathrel{|}h

of
\Phi
. By definition
b\cdot h\in\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell+1}(\Phi)
, and from
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}=\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell+1}(\Phi)
we get
b\cdot h\in\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
. Therefore,
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)

satisfies Property
3
.
∎

In view of
Lemmas

16
and
17
, the
algorithm required by
Lemma

6
presents
itself: it suffices to iteratively compute canonical
representations of every
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
until
reaching a fix-point.
Algorithm

4
performs
this computation.

Algorithm 4

Computes a set spanning a divisibility module

1:

A system of divisibility constraints
\Phi(\boldsymbol{x})=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrel{|}g_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})
and
a primitive polynomial
f
.

2:

A tuple
(c_{1},\dots,c_{m})\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
such that
\{f,\,c_{1}\cdot g_{1},\,\dots,\,c_{m}\cdot g_{m}\}
spans
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)
.

3:

\boldsymbol{v}\coloneqq(0,\dots,0)\in\mathbb{N}^{m}

4:

while

true

do

5:

\boldsymbol{u}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{v}

6:

for

i

in

[1,m]

do

7:

F_{i}\coloneqq\{-f_{i},\,f,\,\boldsymbol{u}[1]\cdot g_{1},\,\dots,\,\boldsymbol{u}[m]\cdot g_{m}\}

8:

K_{i}\coloneqq
basis of the integer kernel of the matrix representing
F_{i}

9:

\boldsymbol{v}[i]\leftarrow\gcd(\text{row of $K_{i}$ corresponding to $-f_{i}$})

10:

if

\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{u}

then

return

\boldsymbol{v}

In a nutshell, during the
\ell
-th iteration (
\ell\geq 1
) of the
while
loop of
line
4
, the variable
\boldsymbol{u}
contains the canonical representation of
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell-1}(\Phi)
, and the algorithm updates the vector
\boldsymbol{v}
with the canonical representation of
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
.
To update the value
\boldsymbol{v}[i]
associated to
g_{i}

the algorithm needs to compute
\gcd\{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}:\lambda\cdot f_{i}\in\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell-1}(\Phi)\}

(line
9
)
.
This is done by finding a finite representation for all the scalars
\lambda
, which is given by those entries corresponding to
-f_{i}
in a basis of the integer kernel of the matrix for the set
F_{i}
defined in line
7
.
As explained in
Section

3.1
,
a set of polynomials
F\coloneqq\{h_{1},\dots,h_{\ell}\}
in variables
x_{1}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}x_{d}
(where
\mathbin{\prec}
is an arbitrary order) can be represented as the matrix
A\in\mathbb{Z}^{(d+1)\times\ell}
in which each column
(a_{d},\dots,a_{1},c)
contains the coefficients and the constant of a distinct element
h
of
F
,
with
a_{i}
being the coefficient of
x_{i}
for
i\in[1,d]
, and
c
being the constant of
h
. This matrix is unique up-to permutation of columns.

It might not be clear for the moment whether
Algorithm

4

runs in
PTIME
: in each iteration, the integer kernel computation
done in line
8
might a priori
increase the bit length of the entries in the canonical
representation by a polynomial factor,
yielding entries of exponential bit length
after polynomially many iterations – an effect similar to
naïve implementations of Gaussian elimination or kernel
computations via suboptimal algorithms for the Hermite
normal form of a matrix. We show later that our worries are
unjustified, as the GCD computed in
line
9
prevents this blow-up. For the
moment, let us formally argue on the correctness of
Algorithm

4
.

Proposition 8
.

Algorithm

4
respects its specification.

Proof.

We write
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}
for the value that the tuple

\boldsymbol{u}
declared in line
5

of
Algorithm

4
takes during the

(\ell+1)
-th iterations of the
while
loop of
line
4
, with
\ell\in\mathbb{N}

and assuming that the
while
loop is iterated
at least
\ell+1
times. We show the following claim:

Claim 9
.

For every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, the tuple
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}
is
the canonical representation of
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
.

Since
Algorithm

4
terminates when

\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell-1}
is found to be equal to
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}
for some
\ell\geq 1
, its correctness
follows directly from
Lemma

16
and
Lemma

17
.
The proof of this claim is by induction on
\ell
.

base case.

We have
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}=(0,\dots,0)\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
, which is the canonical representation of
\textup{M}_{f}^{0}(\Phi)
.

induction step.

By induction hypothesis, let us assume that

\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{m})
is the canonical representation of
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
.
We show that when exiting the
for
loop of line
6
, for every
i\in[1,m]
,
\boldsymbol{v}[i]
equals
v_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\gcd\{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}:\lambda\cdot f_{i}\in\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)\}
.
Thanks to the declaration of line
5
,
this implies that
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell+1}
is the canonical representation of
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell+1}(\Phi)
.
Since
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{m})
is the canonical representation of
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
, by
Lemma

16
we have
\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)=\mathbb{Z}f+\mathbb{Z}(v_{1}\cdot g_{1})+\dots+\mathbb{Z}(v_{m}\cdot g_{m})
.
Therefore,
v_{i}^{\prime}=\gcd\{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}:\lambda\cdot f_{i}=\mu_{0}\cdot f+\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{i}\cdot(v_{i}\cdot g_{i})\text{ for some }\mu_{0},\dots,\mu_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.
The set of tuples
(\lambda,\mu_{0},\dots,\mu_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m+2}
such that
\lambda\cdot f_{i}=\mu_{0}\cdot f+\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{i}\cdot(v_{i}\cdot g_{i})

corresponds to the solutions to the system of equations
A\cdot(\lambda,\mu_{0},\dots,\mu_{m})=\boldsymbol{0}
over the integers,
where
A
is the matrix representing the set
\{-f_{i},\,f,\,v_{i}\cdot g_{1},\,\dots,\,\boldsymbol{v}_{m}\cdot g_{m}\}
, i.e.,
F_{i}
in
line
7
.
This set corresponds to
\ker(A)
, and so can be finitely represented with an integer kernel basis, i.e.,
K_{i}
in line
8
. Computing
v_{i}^{\prime}
only requires to compute the
GCD of the row of
K_{i}
corresponding to the variable
\lambda
of
-f_{i}
. This is exactly how
\boldsymbol{v}[i]
is defined in line
9
.
∎

We move to the runtime analysis
of
Algorithm

4
. We need the following lemma
studying the growth of the GCDs of the rows of bases
K
of
\ker(A)

when columns of
A
are
scaled by positive integers.
In the lemma below,
\textup{diag}(c_{1},\dots,c_{d})
stands for the
d\times d
diagonal matrix having
c_{1},\dots,c_{d}
in the main diagonal.

Lemma 18
.

Consider a matrix
A\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times d}
of rank

r
, integers
c_{1},\dots,c_{d}>0
, and let
K,K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d\times(d-r)}
be bases of the integer kernels of
A

and
A^{\prime}\coloneqq A\cdot\textup{diag}(c_{1},\dots,c_{d})
, respectively. For
every
i\in[1,d]
,

1.

if
\gcd(K[i])=0
then
\gcd(K^{\prime}[i])=0
, and

2.

if
\gcd(K[i])>0
then
\gcd(K^{\prime}[i])\neq 0
and
\gcd(K^{\prime}[i])
divides

\textup{lcm}(c_{1},\dots,c_{d})\cdot\gcd(K[i])
.

Proof.

Note that
A^{\prime}
is the matrix
obtained from
A
by scaling the
j
-th column of
A

by
c_{j}
(
j\in[1,d]
).
Let
i\in[1,d]
and
(M,J)\in\{(A,K),(A^{\prime},K^{\prime})\}
. By definition of kernel,

{\{J\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda}:\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\}}=\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:M\cdot\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{0}\}
.
This fact has three direct consequences:

1.

if
\gcd(J[i])=0
, then no vector
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
satisfies both
x_{i}\neq 0
and
M\cdot\boldsymbol{x}=0
,

2.

if
\gcd(J[i])>0
, then there is
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that
x_{i}=\gcd(J[i])
and
M\cdot\boldsymbol{x}=0
,

3.

if
\gcd(J[i])>0
, then for every
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
satisfying
M\cdot\boldsymbol{x}=0
we have

\gcd(J[i])\mathrel{|}x_{i}
.

Items

1
and
2
in the statement of the lemma are derived from these three properties.

Proof of
(
1
): By
contrapositive, assume that
\gcd(K^{\prime}[i])\neq 0
.
Hence,
\gcd(K^{\prime}[i])>0
and by

Item

2
there is
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that
x_{i}=\gcd(K^{\prime}[i])
and
A^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{0}
. Let

\boldsymbol{y}\coloneqq(c_{1}\cdot x_{1},\dots,c_{d}\cdot x_{d})
. We have
A\cdot\boldsymbol{y}=A\cdot(\textup{diag}(c_{1},\dots,c_{d})\cdot\boldsymbol{x})=(A\cdot\textup{diag}(c_{1},\dots,c_{d}))\cdot\boldsymbol{x}=A^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{0}
. Since
c_{i}>0
we have
c_{i}\cdot x_{i}\neq 0
, which together with
A\cdot\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{0}
implies
\gcd(K[i])\neq 0
by

Item

1
.

Proof of
(
2
): Suppose

\gcd(K[i])>0
. By

Item

2
, there is
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
A\cdot\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{0}
and
x_{i}=\gcd(K[i])
. Define
C\coloneqq\textup{lcm}(c_{1},\dots,c_{d})
and
\boldsymbol{y}\coloneqq(\frac{C}{c_{1}}\cdot x_{1},\dots,\frac{C}{c_{d}}\cdot x_{d})
. Note that
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is well-defined,
since
c_{1},\dots,c_{d}>0
. Moreover,

\frac{C}{c_{i}}\cdot x_{i}=\frac{C}{c_{i}}\cdot\gcd(K[i])>0
. We have,

\displaystyle A^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{y}

\displaystyle\textstyle\displaystyle=A^{\prime}\cdot(\textup{diag}(\frac{C}{c_{1}},\dots,\frac{C}{c_{d}})\cdot\boldsymbol{x})\ =\ (A\cdot\textup{diag}(c_{1},\dots,c_{d}))\cdot(\textup{diag}(\frac{C}{c_{1}},\dots,\frac{C}{c_{d}})\cdot\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\textstyle\displaystyle=A\cdot(\textup{diag}(c_{1},\dots,c_{d})\cdot\textup{diag}(\frac{C}{c_{1}},\dots,\frac{C}{c_{d}}))\cdot\boldsymbol{x}\ =\ C\cdot A\cdot\boldsymbol{x}\ =\ \boldsymbol{0}.

Then, by

Item

1
,
\gcd(K^{\prime}[i])>0
, which in turn implies that
\gcd(K^{\prime}[i])\mathrel{|}\frac{C}{c_{i}}\cdot x_{i}
, directly from

Item

3
. Therefore,

\gcd(K^{\prime}[i])
divides
\textup{lcm}(c_{1},\dots,c_{d})\cdot\gcd(K[i])
.
∎

We are now ready to discuss the runtime
of
Algorithm

4
.

Proposition 9
.

Algorithm

4
runs in
PTIME
, and on an
input
(\Phi,f)
such that
\Phi=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}\mathrel{|}g_{i}

it returns a vector
\boldsymbol{v}
satisfying

{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{v}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq((m+3)\cdot({|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+2))^{(m+3)^{3}}
.

Proof.

As done in the proof of
Proposition

8
,
let
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
be the value that the
tuple
\boldsymbol{u}
declared in
line
5
takes during the

(\ell+1)
-th iteration of the
while
of
line
4
, with
\ell\in\mathbb{N}

and assuming that the
while
loop is iterated
at least
\ell+1
times. Similarly, given
j\in[1,m]
, let
F_{\ell,j}
and
K_{\ell,j}
be
the set of polynomial and matrix declared in
lines
7

and
8
, respectively, during
the
(\ell+1)
-th iteration of the
while

loop and at the end of the iteration of the

for
loop of line
7

where the index variable
i
takes value
j
. Lastly,
following the code in line
9
, we
define
v_{\ell,j}\coloneqq\gcd(\text{row of
$K_{\ell,j}$ corresponding to $-f_{j}$})
. A few preliminary remarks that follow
directly form the definitions above:

For the runtime of the algorithm, first consider the
case where
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)\cap\mathbb{Z}f_{j}=\{0\}
for
every
j\in[1,m]
, which implies
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)=\mathbb{Z}f
, by definition of divisibility module. Focus on
the first execution of the body of the
while

loop. Since
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}=(0,\dots,0)
, for every
j\in[1,m]
,
F_{0,j}=\{-f_{j},\,f\}
. Since
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)\cap\mathbb{Z}f_{j}=\{0\}
, the row of
K_{0,j}

corresponding to
-f_{j}
contains only zeros. This implies
\boldsymbol{v}=(0,\dots,0)=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
in line
10
, and
Algorithm

4

returns
(0,\dots,0)
after a single
iteration of the
while
.

Consider now the case where
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)\cap\mathbb{Z}f_{j}\neq\emptyset
for some
j\in[1,m]
. Note that
this implies
f_{j}=a\cdot f
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
and
j\in[1,m]
, hence
\langle f\rangle\leq\mathrm{poly}(\langle\Phi\rangle)
. This allows us to bound the
size of the output of
Algorithm

4

in terms of
\Phi
, hiding factors that depend
on
f
(as done in the statement of the
proposition). A few auxiliary definitions are handy (
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in[1,m]
):

•

We associate to
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}
the vector

\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\ell}\in\{0,1\}^{m}
given by

\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\ell}[i]=1
iff
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}[i]\neq 0
, for every
i\in[1,m]
.

•

We associate to
F_{\ell,j}
the set
\widehat{F}_{\ell,j}\coloneqq\{-f_{j},\,f,\,\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\ell}[1]\cdot g_{1},\,\dots,\,\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\ell}[m]\cdot g_{m}\}
.

•

We associate to
K_{\ell,j}
a basis
\widehat{K}_{\ell,j}
for the integer kernel of the matrix representing
\widehat{F}_{\ell,j}
.

•

We associate to
v_{\ell,j}
the integer
\widehat{v}_{\ell,j}\coloneqq\gcd(\text{row of $\widehat{K}_{\ell,j}$ corresponding to $-f_{j}$})
.

In a nutshell,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\ell}
“forgets” the magnitude of the integers stored in
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}
, keeping only whether their value was
0
or not. The other objects defined above reflect this change at the level of matrices, kernels and GCDs.
Up to permutation of columns, the matrix representing
F_{\ell,j}
can be obtained by multiplying the matrix of
\widehat{F}_{\ell,j}
by a diagonal matrix having in the main diagonal (a permutation of)
(1,1,\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}[1],\dots,\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}[m])
.
From the definition of
\widehat{K}_{\ell,j}
and
by
Lemma

18
, we conclude that

if
\widehat{v}_{\ell,j}=0

then
v_{\ell,j}=0
,
and if
\widehat{v}_{\ell,j}\neq 0
then
v_{\ell,j}\neq 0
and

v_{\ell,j}
divides

\textup{lcm}(\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell})\cdot\widehat{v}_{\ell,j}
.

(
\dagger
)

Recall that the matrix representing
\widehat{F}_{\ell,j}
has
d+1
rows and
m+2
columns.
Since
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\widehat{F}_{\ell,j}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}
for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in[1,m]
, by
Corollary

2

there an integer

N\in[2,\,{((m+3)\cdot({|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+2))^{(m+3)}}]

such that
N
is greater
than
\widehat{v}_{\ell,j}
, for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in[1,m]
. We use (

†

‣
C.1
) above to bound the
number of iterations and magnitude of the entries
of
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}
during the procedure. We show that

1.

\max_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}(\textup{lcm}(\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}))=\max_{\ell=0}^{m}(\textup{lcm}(\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}))\leq N^{m^{3}}
and for every

j\in[1,m]
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{m}[j]\leq N^{m^{2}}
, and

2.

the

while
loop of
line
4
is iterated at most

m^{3}\cdot\log_{2}(N)+m
many times.

In Item (
1
) above we
slightly abused our notation, as
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}
is
undefined for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
greater or equal than
the number of iterations of the
while
loop
performed by the algorithm. In these cases, we
postulate
\textup{lcm}(\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell})=0
in order to make
the equivalence in Item (
1
)
well-defined. From the bound
N\leq((m+3)\cdot({|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+2))^{(m+3)}
,
Items (
1
)
and (
2
) imply
that
Algorithm

4
runs in
PTIME
and outputs a vector
\boldsymbol{v}
with
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\boldsymbol{v}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq((m+3)\cdot({|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+2))^{(m+3)^{3}}
;
proving the proposition.

Proof of
(
1
):
Informally, Item (
1
) states
that
\textup{lcm}(\boldsymbol{u})
is always bounded by
N^{m^{2}}
,
and that
\textup{lcm}(\boldsymbol{u})
achieves its maximum at most
after the first
m
iterations of the
while

loop. We start by proving that
{\max_{\ell=0}^{m}(\textup{lcm}(\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}))\leq N^{m^{3}}}
and that for
every
j\in[1,m]
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{m}[j]\leq N^{m^{2}}
This
is done by induction on
\ell\in[1,m]
, by showing
that (whenever defined)
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}
is such that, for
every
j\in[1,m]
, if
{\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}[j]\neq 0}
then

{\widehat{v}_{\ell-1,j}\neq 0}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}[j]
divides
\big(\widehat{v}_{\ell-1,j}\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{\ell-2}\textup{lcm}(\widehat{v}_{i,1},\dots,\widehat{v}_{i,m})\big)
.
Note that then
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}[j]\leq N^{m(\ell-1)+1}
,
since
N
is an upper bound on every

\widehat{v}_{\ell,j}
, and thus for
\ell=m
we
get
\boldsymbol{u}_{m}[j]\leq N^{m^{2}}
and
\textup{lcm}(\boldsymbol{u}_{m})\leq N^{m^{3}}
, as required. Below, let
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{m})
. Note that, from line
9
of the algorithm,
if
\ell\geq 1
, then
c_{j}=v_{\ell-1,j}
for every
j\in[1,m]
.

base case
\ell=1
.

From
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}=(0,\dots,0)
we have
F_{0,j}=\widehat{F}_{0,j}=\{-f_{j},f\}
for every
j\in[1,m]
. This implies
\widehat{v}_{0,j}=v_{0,j}
.
From
c_{j}=v_{0,j}
, we conclude that
c_{j}=\widehat{v}_{0,j}
, completing the base case.

induction step
\ell\geq 2
.

Let
j\in[1,m]
such that
c_{j}\neq 0
.
From (

†

‣
C.1
) and
c_{j}=v_{\ell-1,j}
, we get

\widehat{v}_{\ell-1,j}\neq 0
and

c_{j}\mathrel{|}(\textup{lcm}(\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell-1})\cdot\widehat{v}_{\ell-1,j})
. Let
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell-1}=(c_{1}^{*},\dots,c_{m}^{*})
. From the induction
hypothesis, for every
{k\in[1,m]}
, if
c_{k}^{*}\neq 0
then
\widehat{v}_{\ell-2,k}\neq 0

and
c_{k}^{*}\mathrel{|}\big(\widehat{v}_{\ell-2,k}\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{\ell-3}\textup{lcm}(\widehat{v}_{i,1},\dots,\widehat{v}_{i,m})\big)
.
Therefore,

\textup{lcm}(\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell-1})\ \mathrel{|}\ \textup{lcm}\big((\widehat{v}_{\ell-2,1}\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{\ell-3}\textup{lcm}(\widehat{v}_{i,1},\dots,\widehat{v}_{i,m})),\dots,(\widehat{v}_{\ell-2,m}\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{\ell-3}\textup{lcm}(\widehat{v}_{i,1},\dots,\widehat{v}_{i,m}))\big).

From the equivalence
\textup{lcm}(a\cdot b,c\cdot b)=\textup{lcm}(a,c)\cdot b
,
the right-hand side of the divisibility above
equals

\prod_{i=0}^{\ell-2}\textup{lcm}(\widehat{v}_{i,1},\dots,\widehat{v}_{i,m})
.
Then, the fact that
c_{j}
divides
\big(\widehat{v}_{\ell-1,j}\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{\ell-2}\textup{lcm}(\widehat{v}_{i,1},\dots,\widehat{v}_{i,m})\big)

follows
directly from
c_{j}\mathrel{|}(\textup{lcm}(\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell-1})\cdot\widehat{v}_{\ell-1,j})
.

To complete the proof of (
1
),
we now show that
\max_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}(\textup{lcm}(\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}))=\max_{\ell=0}^{m}(\textup{lcm}(\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}))
.
Directly from
9

in the proof of
Proposition

8
,
we have that for every
\ell\geq 1
, the vector

\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}
is the canonical representation of

\textup{M}_{f}^{\ell}(\Phi)
. We have,

1.

for every

j\in[1,m]
, if
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}[j]\neq 0
then

\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell+1}[j]
divides
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}[j]

(assuming both
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell+1}
defined).

This follows directly from
Lemma

15
.

2.

If
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell+1}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell+2}

are defined, and
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell+1}
have the same zero entries, then
also
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell+2}
have the same
zero entries.

Indeed, in this case
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\ell}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\ell+1}
which implies
\widehat{v}_{\ell,j}=\widehat{v}_{\ell+1,j}
for every
j\in[1,m]
. Now, if
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell+2}[j]\neq 0
then
v_{\ell+1,j}\neq 0
and so
\widehat{v}_{\ell+1,j}\neq 0
by (

†

‣
C.1
). Then
\widehat{v}_{\ell,j}\neq 0
, and again by (

†

‣
C.1
) we get
v_{\ell,j}\neq 0
.
If instead
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell+2}[j]=0
, then
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}[j]=0
follows
from
Lemma

15
.

Since
\boldsymbol{u}
is a tuple with
m
entries,
Item (
2
) above
ensures that every
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{r}

with
\ell,r\geq m
share the same zero entries.
Item (
1
) states
instead that every non-zero entry of
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}

upper bounds the corresponding entry of
\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell+r}
,
for every
r\in\mathbb{N}
, and that this latter entry is always non-zero.
Together,
Items (
1
)
and (
2
)
imply that
\max_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}(\textup{lcm}(\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}))=\max_{\ell=0}^{m}(\textup{lcm}(\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}))
.

Proof of
(
2
):
Assume that the
while
loop iterates at least
m+1

times (otherwise (
2
)
is trivially satisfied).
From (
2
),
the vector
\boldsymbol{u}_{m}
such that
\boldsymbol{u}_{m}[j]\leq N^{m^{2}}
for every

j\in[1,m]
. As we have just discussed above,
by Item (
2
), every
subsequent
\boldsymbol{u}_{m+r}
with
r\in\mathbb{N}
has the
same zero entries as
\boldsymbol{u}_{m}
. Whenever
\boldsymbol{u}_{m+r}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{m+r+1}
are both defined
(meaning in particular that
\boldsymbol{u}_{m+r}\neq\boldsymbol{u}_{m+r+1}
), there must be
j\in[1,m]
such that

\boldsymbol{u}_{m+r}[j]\neq\boldsymbol{u}_{m+r+1}[j]
, and
moreover by
Item (
1
),
\boldsymbol{u}_{m+r+1}[i]
divides
\boldsymbol{u}_{m+r}[i]
for every
i\in[1,m]
, which in particular implies that
\boldsymbol{u}_{m+r+1}[j]\leq\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{m+r}[j]}{2}
.
Therefore, the product of all non-zero entries of
\boldsymbol{u}
(at least) halves at each
iteration of the
while
loop after the
m
-th
one. By (
1
), for every
j\in[1,m]
we have
\boldsymbol{u}_{m}[j]\leq N^{m^{2}}
, so the product of all
non-zero entries in
\boldsymbol{u}_{m}
is bounded by
N^{m^{3}}
. We
conclude that the number of iterations of the

while
loop after the
m
-th one is bounded
by
\log_{2}(N^{m^{3}})=m^{3}\cdot\log_{2}(N)
; i.e.,
m^{3}\cdot\log_{2}(N)+m
many iterations overall.
∎

C.2
Closing a system of divisibility constraints under the elimination property

See
7

Proof.

The algorithm is simple to state:

1:

F\coloneqq\{f\text{ primitive }:\text{$a\cdot f$ is in the left-hand side of a divisibility of $\Phi$, for some $a\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}$}\}

2:

for

f\in F

do

3:

\boldsymbol{v}\coloneqq(c_{1},\dots,c_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
s.t.
\{f,c_{1}\cdot g_{1},\dots,c_{m}\cdot g_{m}\}
spans
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)

\triangleright

Lemma

6

4:

H

\coloneqq
HNF of the matrix representing
\{f,c_{1}\cdot g_{1},\dots,c_{m}\cdot g_{m}\}

\triangleright

Proposition

7

5:

\Phi

\leftarrow

\Phi
purged of all divisibilities of the form
f\mathrel{|}g
for some polynomial
g

6:

for

(a_{d},\dots,a_{1},a_{0})
non-zero column of
H

do

7:

\Phi

\leftarrow

\Phi\land(f\mathrel{|}a_{d}\cdot x_{d}+\dots+a_{1}\cdot x_{1}+a_{0})

8:

return

\Phi

Below, let
\Psi
be the system returned by the algorithm on input
\Phi
.

The fact that
\Psi
has the elimination property follows from properties of
the Hermite normal form. Consider
F
defined as in
line
1
, and
f\in F
. Starting from the matrix
A\in\mathbb{Z}^{(d+1)\times(m+1)}
representing the spanning set
S\coloneqq\{f,c_{1}\cdot g_{1},\dots,c_{m}\cdot g_{m}\}
computed in line
3
, by

Proposition

6
we conclude that
H
in
line
4
spans
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)
.
Moreover, by properties of the HNF, all non-zero columns of
H
are linearly independent, hence the
for
loop in line
6
is adding divisibilities
f\mathrel{|}h_{1},\dots,f\mathrel{|}h_{\ell}
where
h_{1},\dots,h_{\ell}
is a basis of
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)
; and
\ell\leq m+1
. Note that line
5
has previously removed all divisibilities of the form
f\mathrel{|}g
. Hence, in
\Psi
only the divisibilities
f\mathrel{|}h_{1},\dots,f\mathrel{|}h_{\ell}
have
f
as a left-hand side. Recall now that each column
(a_{d},\dots,a_{1},c)
of the matrix
A
contains the coefficients and the constant of a distinct element
h\in S
,
with
a_{i}
being the coefficient of
x_{i}
for
i\in[1,d]
, and
c
being the constant of
h
. Again since
H
is in HNF, it is lower triangular, and the pivot of each non-zero column is strictly below the pivot of the column before it. Following the order
x_{1}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}x_{d}
, this allows us to conclude that, for every
k\in[0,d]
, the family
\{g_{1},\dots,g_{j}\}\coloneqq\{g:\textup{LV}(g)\preceq x_{k}\text{ and }f\mathrel{|}g\text{ appears in }\Psi\}
is such that
g_{1},\dots,g_{j}
are linearly independent polynomials forming a basis for
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)\cap\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\dots,x_{k}]
; i.e.,
\Psi
has the elimination property. We also note that, by virtue of the updates done in
7
,
Items

1
and
2
in the statement of
Lemma

7
directly follow.

The fact that
\Psi
and
\Phi
are equivalent both over
\mathbb{Z}
and for solutions modulo a prime follows from
Items

1
and
2
together with the following property of divisibility modules:
given a system of divisibility constraints
\Phi^{\prime}
and a primitive term
f
,

•

for every
\boldsymbol{a}
integer solution of
\Phi^{\prime}
and every
g\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi^{\prime})
,
f(\boldsymbol{a})
divides
g(\boldsymbol{a})
,

•

for every
p\in\mathbb{P}
,
\boldsymbol{b}
solution of
\Phi^{\prime}
modulo
p
and every
g\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi^{\prime})
,
v_{p}(f(\boldsymbol{b}))\leq v_{p}(g(\boldsymbol{b}))
.

Here, note that given polynomials
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
with
v_{p}(f(\boldsymbol{b}))\leq v_{p}(g_{1}(\boldsymbol{b}))
and
{v_{p}(f(\boldsymbol{b}))\leq v_{p}(g_{2}(\boldsymbol{b}))}
we have
v_{p}(f(\boldsymbol{b}))\leq v_{p}(a_{1}\cdot g_{1}(\boldsymbol{b})+a_{2}\cdot g_{2}(\boldsymbol{b}))
for every
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
, as the
p
-adic evaluation satisfies
v_{p}(x\cdot y)=v_{p}(x)+v_{p}(y)
and
\min(v_{p}(x),v_{p}(y))\leq v_{p}(x+y)
, for all
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Let us now move to the bounds on
\Psi
stated in
Item

3
.
Directly from
\#F\leq m
and the fact that
H
is lower triangular we conclude that at most
m\cdot(d+1)
divisibilities are added, and so
\Psi
has at most
m\cdot(d+2)
divisibilities.
We analyze the norm of
\Psi
.
It suffices to consider a single
f\in F
. By definition,
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}f{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}
, and
from
Lemma

6
, the infinity norm of the matrix
A
representing
\{f,c_{1}\cdot g_{1},\dots,c_{m}\cdot g_{m}\}
is bounded by

((m+3)\cdot({|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+2))^{(m+3)^{3}}\cdot{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}
.
Note that
A
has
d+1
many rows.
By
Proposition

7
, the matrix
H
in line
4
is such that

\displaystyle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}H{|\mkern-2.0mu|}

\displaystyle\leq((d+1)\cdot{|\mkern-2.0mu|}A{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+1)^{O(d)}

\displaystyle\leq\Big((d+1)\cdot\big(((m+3)\cdot({|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+2))^{(m+3)^{3}}\cdot{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\big)+1\Big)^{O(d)}

\displaystyle\leq(d+1)^{O(d)}(m+{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+2)^{O(m^{3}d)}.

From the updates done in line
7
, we conclude that
{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Psi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\leq(d+1)^{O(d)}(m+{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}+2)^{O(m^{3}d)}
.
∎

See
8

Proof.

Let
f
be a primitive polynomial. By definition of
divisibility module, the lemma is true as soon as we prove

1.

f\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y}))
,

2.

\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y}))
is a

\mathbb{Z}
-module, and

3.

for every
divisibility
g^{\prime}\mathrel{|}h^{\prime}
(with
g^{\prime}
non-zero) appearing
in
\Phi(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
, if
b\cdot g^{\prime}\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y}))
for some
b\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
b\cdot h^{\prime}\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y}))
.

Indeed, by definition
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y}))
is the smallest set fulfilling these three
properties, and therefore it must then be included in

\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y}))
.

The first two properties trivially follow by definition
of
\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y}))
, hence let
us focus on Property (
3
).
Consider a divisibility
g^{\prime}\mathrel{|}h^{\prime}
appearing in

\Phi(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
and such that
b\cdot g^{\prime}\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y}))
. By
definition of
\Phi(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
, there is
a divisibility
g\mathrel{|}h
appearing in
\Phi
such that

{(g\mathrel{|}h)[\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathbin{/}\boldsymbol{x}]=(g^{\prime}\mathrel{|}h^{\prime})}
. We split the proof depending on whether
g

is a primitive polynomial.

g
is not a primitive polynomial.

By
Item

1

in
Lemma

7
the
divisibility
g\mathrel{|}h
occurs in
\Psi
. So,
g^{\prime}\mathrel{|}h^{\prime}
is in
\Psi(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
and
directly by definition of divisibility module,
b\cdot h^{\prime}\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y}))
.

g
is a primitive polynomial.

Let

\widetilde{g}
and
c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
be
such that
g^{\prime}=c^{\prime}\cdot\widetilde{g}
.
By
Item

2

in
Lemma

7
, since
g\mathrel{|}h
appears in
\Phi
,

h\in\textup{M}_{g}(\Psi)
. By the elimination property
of
\Psi
, there are divisibilities
g\mathrel{|}h_{1},\dots,g\mathrel{|}h_{k}
such that
h=\lambda_{1}\cdot h_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{k}\cdot h_{k}
for some

\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
.
Every divisibility
(g\mathrel{|}h_{i})[\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathbin{/}\boldsymbol{x}]
with
i\in[1,k]
appears in

\Psi(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
. Since
g^{\prime}=g(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
and
b\cdot g^{\prime}\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y}))
we have

b\cdot h_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y}))
for
every
i\in[1,k]
. Note that
h^{\prime}=h(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})=\lambda_{1}\cdot h_{1}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})+\dots+\lambda_{k}\cdot h_{k}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
, and therefore since the divisibility
module is a
\mathbb{Z}
-module,
b\cdot h^{\prime}\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y}))
.
∎

Appendix D
Bounding the number of difficult primes

In this appendix, we establish
Lemmas

3
,
9
and
4
.

See
3

Proof.

We remark that
p
not dividing any coefficients nor
constants appearing in the left-hand sides of
\Phi

implies that all the left-hand sides are non-zero. We show
that the system of non-congruences defined by
f_{i}\not\equiv 0\pmod{p}
for every
i\in[1,m]
, admits a
solution
\boldsymbol{b}
. This solution can clearly be taken with
entries in
[0,p-1]
. Furthermore,
v_{p}(f_{i}(\boldsymbol{b}))=0

and
f_{i}(\boldsymbol{b})\neq 0
for every
i\in[1,m]
, and
therefore
\boldsymbol{b}
is a solution for
\Phi
modulo
p
no
matter the values of
v_{p}(g_{i}(\boldsymbol{b}))
(
i\in[1,m]
).

Consider an arbitrary ordering
x_{1}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}x_{d}
on the variables in
\boldsymbol{x}
. We construct
\boldsymbol{b}

by induction on
k\in[0,d]
. At the
k
-th step of the
induction we deal with the linear terms
h
having

{\textup{LV}(h)=x_{k}}
. Below, we write
F_{0}
for the set of
the left-hand sides in
\Phi
that are constant
polynomials, and
F_{k}
with
k\in[1,d]
for the set of
the left-hand sides
f
in
\Phi
such that
\textup{LV}(f)\preceq x_{k}
.

base case:
k=0
.

Every
f\in F_{0}
is a non-zero integer. Then,
f\not\equiv 0\pmod{p}
directly follows from the
hypothesis that
p
does not divide any constant
appearing in the left-hand sides of
\Phi
.

induction step:
k\geq 1
.

From the induction hypothesis, there is
\boldsymbol{b}_{k-1}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{k-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k-1}
such
that for every
f\in F_{k-1}
,
f(\boldsymbol{b}_{k-1})\not\equiv 0\pmod{p}
. We find a value
b_{k}
for

x_{k}
so that the following system of
non-congruences is satisfied

\displaystyle f(\boldsymbol{b}_{k-1},x_{k})\not\equiv 0\pmod{p}

\displaystyle f\in F_{k}\setminus F_{k-1}.

Linear polynomials
f
in
F_{k}\setminus F_{k-1}

are of the form
f(\boldsymbol{x})=f^{\prime}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1})+c_{f}\cdot x_{k}
. Since by hypothesis
p\nmid c_{f}
,
we consider the multiplicative inverse
c_{f}^{-1}
of

c_{f}
modulo
p
, and rewrite the above system as

x_{k}\not\equiv-c_{f}^{-1}\cdot f^{\prime}
for every
f\in F_{k}\setminus F_{k-1}
. This system as a solution
directly from the fact that
p>m\geq\#(F_{k}\setminus F_{k-1})
.
∎

Before proving
Lemmas

9
and
4
,
we need the following result on system of divisibility constraints with the elimination property, that will later be used also in the proof of
4
.

Lemma 19
.

Let

\Phi(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})
be a system of divisibility with
the elimination property for the order
x_{1}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}x_{d}
. For every primitive term
f
and
j\in[1,d]
, the set
F\coloneqq\{g:{(f\mathrel{|}g)}\text{ appears in }\Phi\}
has at most one
element with leading variable
x_{j}
.

Proof.

If
f
does not appear in the left-hand side of a divisibility of
\Phi
,
then
F=\emptyset
and the lemma holds. Suppose
f
in a left-hand side.
For simplicity, let us define
x_{0}\coloneqq\bot
. By
definition, for every
{k\in[0,d]}
, the elimination
property forces
\{g_{1},\dots,g_{\ell}\}\coloneqq\{g:{\textup{LV}(g)\preceq x_{k}}\text{ and }{f\mathrel{|}g}\text{ appears in }\Phi\}
to be such that

g_{1},\dots,g_{\ell}
are linearly independent
polynomials forming a basis for
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)\cap\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\dots,x_{k}]
. Given
k\in[0,d]
, let us write

F_{k}\coloneqq\{g:\textup{LV}(g)\preceq x_{k}\text{ and }(f\mid g)\text{ appear in }\Phi\}
. For
j\in[1,d]
, by the elimination property,
F_{j-1}
and

F_{j}
are sets of linearly independent vectors, that
respectively generates
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)\cap\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\dots,x_{j-1}]
and
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)\cap\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\dots,x_{j}]
.
To conclude the proof, we show by induction on
j
that the set

F_{j}
has at most one element with leading variable
x_{j}
.

base case
j=0
.

In this case
F_{0}
only contains constant polynomials (and might be empty, in that case it generates the subspace
\{0\}
). By elimination property,
F
is a set of linearly independent vectors, hence
F_{0}
contains at most one element.

induction step
j\geq 1
.

Ad absurdum
, suppose there are two distinct
g_{1},g_{2}\in F_{j}\setminus F_{j-1}
such that
\textup{LV}(g_{1})=\textup{LV}(g_{2})=x_{j}
. By definition of
S
-polynomial,
S(g_{1},g_{2})\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)\cap\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\dots,x_{j-1}]
. Since
F_{j-1}
generates
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)\cap\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\dots,x_{j-1}]
, there is a sequence of integers
(\lambda_{h})_{h\in F_{j-1}}
such that

\sum_{h\in F_{j-1}}\lambda_{h}\cdot h=S(g_{1},g_{2})
.
However,
F_{j-1}\cup\{g_{1},g_{2}\}\subseteq F_{j}
(by definition) and
F_{j}
is a set of linearly independent vectors. Therefore, every
\lambda_{h}
above must be
0
, and we obtain
S(g_{1},g_{2})=0
, i.e.,
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are linearly dependent, in contradiction with
g_{1},g_{2}\in F_{j}
.
∎

See
9

Proof.

Consider a primitive term
f
. If
f
is not a primitive part of any
f_{i}
, with
i\in[1,m]
,
then
\mathrm{S}_{f}(\Phi)=\textup{terms}(\Phi)
and so
\mathrm{S}_{f}(\Phi)
is included in any
\mathrm{S}_{f^{\prime}}(\Phi)
where
f^{\prime}
is a primitive part of a left-hand side of
\Phi
. Hence, we can upper bound
\#\Delta(\Phi)
and
\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle
by only looking at these primitive parts.

Proof of

1
:
For
f
primitive part of some polynomials in a left-hand side of
\Phi
, the elements of

\mathrm{S}_{f}(\Phi)
have the form

S\big(g_{k},S(g_{k-1},\dots S(g_{1},h))\big)

where
h\in\textup{terms}(\Phi)
and
f\mid g_{i}
is a divisibility in
\Phi
, for all
i\in[1,k]
. Moreover, each

g_{i}
has the same leading variable as

h_{i}\coloneqq S(g_{i-1},S(g_{i-2},\dots,S(g_{1},h)))
.
Since
\Phi
has the elimination property, by
Lemma

19
,
given
h_{i}
there is at most one
g

such that
f\mathrel{|}g
and
\textup{LV}(g)=\textup{LV}(h_{i})
; that is
g_{i}
.
Therefore, each element of
\mathrm{S}_{f}(\Phi)

can be characterized
by a pair
(k,h)
where
h\in\textup{terms}(\Phi)
and
k\in[0,d+1]
, i.e.,
\#\mathrm{S}_{f}(\Phi)\leq\#\textup{terms}(\Phi)\cdot(d+2)\leq 2\cdot m\cdot(d+2)
, since
\#\textup{terms}(\Phi)\leq 2\cdot m
.
The number of
f
to be considered is bounded by
m
, i.e., the number of left-hand sides, which means

\#\Delta(\Phi)\leq 2\cdot m^{2}(d+2)
.

Proof of

2
:
Recall that
\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}f{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle
is the maximum bit length of a coefficient or constant of a polynomial
f
, and that
\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}R{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle=\max_{f\in R}\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}f{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle
for a finite set
R
of polynomials. By examinating the definition of
S
-polynomial, we get that for every
f
and
g
,

{\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}S(f,g){|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle}\leq{\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}f{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle}+{\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}g{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle}+1
. Let
f
be a primitive polynomial. As discussed in the proof of (
1
), an element of
\mathrm{S}_{f}(\Phi)
is of the form
S\big(g_{k},S(g_{k-1},\dots S(g_{1},h))\big)
, where
h\in\textup{terms}(\Phi)
,
f\mid g_{i}
is a divisibility in
\Phi
, for all
i\in[1,k]
, and
k\leq d+1
.
Then,
\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}S\big(g_{k},S(g_{k-1},\dots S(g_{1},h))\big){|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle\leq\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}h{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle+\big(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}g_{i}{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle\big)+k
.
We conclude that
\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Delta(\Phi){|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle\leq(d+2)\cdot(\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle+1)
.
∎

See
4

Proof.

We first analyse
\log_{2}(\Pi\mathbb{P}(\Phi))
.
Recall that
\mathbb{P}(\Phi)
is the set of those primes
p
such that
either (i)
p\leq m
or (ii)
p
divide a coefficient
or a constant of a left-hand side of
\Phi
.
The product of the primes satisfying (i) is bounded by
m!\leq m^{m}
.
The product of the primes satisfying (ii) is bounded by the product of
the coefficients or the constants in the left-hand sides of

\Phi
, which is at most

{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}^{m\cdot(d+1)}
.
From these two bounds,
we obtain the bound on
\log_{2}(\Pi\mathbb{P}(\Phi))
stated in the lemma.

Let us analyse
\log_{2}(\Pi\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Phi))
.
Without loss of generality, assume that the order

\mathbin{\prec}
is such that
{x_{1}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}x_{d}}
.
We consider the three conditions defining
\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Phi)

separately, and establish upper bounds for each of them.
Recall that the number of primes dividing
n\in\mathbb{Z}
is
bounded by
\log_{2}(n)
, and that
Lemma

9

implies
\#S(\Delta(\Phi))\leq 8\cdot m^{4}(d+2)^{2}
and
\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}S(\Delta(\Phi)){|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle\leq 2\cdot(d+2)\cdot(\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle+1)+1
.

(P1)
:

Directly from the bounds above, the primes satisfying
(P1)

are at most
{8\cdot m^{4}(d+2)^{2}}
, and thus the
\log_{2}
of their product is at most
{8\cdot m^{4}(d+2)^{2}\log_{2}(8\cdot m^{4}(d+2)^{2})}
, which is bounded by
{64\cdot m^{5}(d+2)^{3}}
.

(P2)
:

The product of the primes dividing a coefficient or constant of a polynomial
f
in
S(\Delta(\Phi))
is bounded by the product of these coefficients and constants. There are at most
(d+1)\cdot\#S(\Delta(\Phi))
such coefficients and constants. Therefore, the
\log_{2}
of this product is bounded by
(d+1)\cdot\#S(\Delta(\Phi))\cdot\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}S(\Delta(\Phi)){|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle
, which is bounded by
{16\cdot m^{4}(d+2)^{4}(\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle+2)}
.

(P3)
:

If
f
is a primitive term such that
a\cdot f
does not occur in the left-hand sides of
\Phi
, for any
a\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, then
\mathrm{S}_{f}(\Phi)=\textup{terms}(\Phi)
and
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)=\mathbb{Z}f
, and therefore
\lambda
, if it exists, equals to
1
.
Consider
f
primitive such that
a\cdot f\in\textup{terms}(\Phi)
appears on the left-hand side of a divisibility in
\Phi
, for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, and consider

g\in\mathrm{S}_{f}(\Phi)
. We first compute a bound on the minimal positive
\lambda
such that
\lambda\cdot g\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)
, if such a
\lambda
exists.
Let
x_{j}\coloneqq\textup{LV}(g)
, with
j\in[0,d]
and
x_{0}\coloneqq\bot
.
Consider the set
\{h_{1},\dots,h_{\ell}\}\coloneqq\{h:\textup{LV}(h)\leq\textup{LV}(g)\text{ and }f\mathrel{|}h\text{ is in }\Phi\}
; where
\ell\leq m
.
From the elimination property, this set is a basis for
\textup{M}_{f}(\Phi)\cap\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\dots,x_{j}]
, and therefore
\lambda
exists if and only if
{\mathbb{Z}g\cap\mathbb{Z}h_{1}+\dots+\mathbb{Z}h_{\ell}\neq\{0\}}
.
Then let
K
be a basis for the kernel of the matrix representing the set
\{-g,h_{1},\dots,h_{\ell}\}
. As observed in the context of
Algorithm

4
, if
\lambda
exists then it is the GCD of the row of
K
corresponding to
-g
.
From
Corollary

2
,

\lambda\leq\big(m+3)^{m+3}\max(2,{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|})^{m+2}
.
In the proof of
Lemma

9
we have shown
\#\mathrm{S}_{f}(\Phi)\leq 2\cdot m\cdot(d+2)
, hence the number of pairs
(f,g)
to consider is bounded by
2\cdot m^{2}\cdot(d+2)
.
Similarly to
(P2)
, the product of the primes dividing all
\lambda
s is bounded by the product of these
\lambda
s, which is at most
\big((m+3)^{m+3}\max(2,{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|})^{m+2}\big)^{2\cdot m^{2}\cdot(d+2)}
.
Therefore,
the
\log_{2}
of the product of the primes satisfying
(P3)
is at most

32\cdot m^{4}(d+2)\cdot(\langle{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\Phi{|\mkern-2.0mu|}\rangle+1)
.

Summing up the bounds we have just obtained yield
the bound stated in the lemma.
∎

Appendix E
Theorem

4
: proofs
of
4

and
5

In this section, we prove
4

and
5
, which are required to
establish
Theorem

4
. In the context of
this theorem, recall that
\Psi(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})
is a
formula that is increasing for
(X_{1}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}X_{r})
and has the elimination property
for an order
(\mathbin{\prec})\in(X_{1}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}X_{r})
. Here,
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})
are the variables appearing in
X_{1}
,
ordered as
x_{1}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}x_{d}
, and
\boldsymbol{y}
are
the variables appearing in
\bigcup_{j=2}^{r}X_{j}
. We also
have solutions
\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
for
\Psi
modulo
p
, for
every
p\in\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
, and we have inductively computed
a map
\boldsymbol{\nu}\colon X_{1}\to\mathbb{Z}
the following three
properties:

IH1:

For every
p\in\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
and
x\in X_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\equiv b_{p,x}\pmod{p^{\mu_{p}+1}}
, where

b_{p,x}
is the entry of
\boldsymbol{b}_{p}
corresponding to

x
, and
\mu_{p}\coloneqq\max\{v_{p}(f(\boldsymbol{b}_{p}))\in\mathbb{N}:f\text{ is in the left-hand side of a
divisibility of }\Psi\}
.

IH2:

For every prime
p\notin\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
and for every

h,h^{\prime}\in\Delta(\Psi)
with leading variable in
X_{1}
,
if
S(h,h^{\prime})
is not identically zero, then
p
does not
divide both
h(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}))
and
h^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}))
.

IH3:

h(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}))\neq 0
for every
h\in\Delta(\Psi)
that is non-zero and with
\textup{LV}(h)\in X_{1}
.

The formula
\Psi^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})
considered
in
4

and
5
is defined as
\Psi^{\prime}\coloneqq\Psi[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x:x\in X_{1}]
.

See
4

At first glance,
4
might appear
intuitively true: since the notion of
r
-increasing form is
mainly a property on sets
X_{1}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}X_{r}
of
orders of variables, and during the proof
of
Theorem

4
we are inductively
handling the smallest set
X_{1}
, it might seem trivial that
instantiating the variables in
X_{1}
preserve increasingness
for
X_{2}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}X_{r}
. However, in general,
this is not the case.
To see this, we repropose the example given in
Section

1.3
.
Consider the system of
divisibility constraints
\Psi
in increasing form for the order
u\mathbin{\prec}v\mathbin{\prec}x\mathbin{\prec}y\mathbin{\prec}z
and with the
elimination property for that order:

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\mathrel{|}u+x+y

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\mathrel{|}x

\displaystyle y+2

\displaystyle\mathrel{|}z+1

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\mathrel{|}z\,.

From the first two divisibilities, we have
(u+y)\in\textup{M}_{v}(\Psi)
; i.e.,
(u-2)+(y+2)\in\textup{M}_{v}(\Psi)
.
Therefore, if
u
were to be instantiated as
2
, the
resulting formula
\Psi^{\prime}
would satisfy
(y+2)\in\textup{M}_{v}(\Psi^{\prime})
and hence
(z+1)\in\textup{M}_{v}(\Psi^{\prime})
,
from the third divisibility. Then,
1\in\textup{M}_{v}(\Psi^{\prime})

would follow from the last divisibility, violating the
constraints of the increasing form. Fortunately, due to the
definition of
\mathrm{S}_{f}(\Psi)
,
u=2
contradicts the
property (
IH3:
) kept during the
proof of
Theorem

4
, meaning that the
above issue does not occur in our setting. Indeed, note that

S(y+2,u+x+y)=2-u-x
is in
\mathrm{S}_{v}(\Psi)
, and so is

S(2-u-x,x)=2-u
. Then, (
IH3:
)
forces
2-u\neq 0
, excluding
u=2
as a possible
solution. This observation is the key to
establish
4
.

Given a set
A
of polynomials, an integer
a\in\mathbb{Z}
and a
variable
x
occurring in those polynomials, we define

A[a\mathbin{/}x]\coloneqq\{f(a,\boldsymbol{y}):f(x,\boldsymbol{y})\in A\}
, that is the set obtained by partially evaluating

x
as
a
in all polynomials in
A
. This notion is
extended to sequences of value-variable pairs as

A[a_{i}\mathbin{/}x_{i}:i\in I]
.

Proof of
4
.

To show the statement, we consider an order
\mathbin{\prec}^{\prime}

in
(X_{1}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}X_{r})
. Note that any
order
(X_{2}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}X_{r})
can be
constructed from elements in
(X_{1}\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}X_{r})
by simply forgetting
X_{1}
. Let
\boldsymbol{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{j})
, with
y_{1}\mathbin{\prec}^{\prime}\dots\mathbin{\prec}^{\prime}y_{j}
, be the variables in
\bigcup_{i=2}^{r}X_{i}
. To
simplify the presentation, we denote by
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},\dots

and
f^{\prime},g^{\prime},\dots
integers and polynomials related to

\Psi^{\prime}
, and by
a,b,\dots
and
f,g,\dots
integers and
polynomials related to
\Psi
. By definition of
increasing form, we need to establish that for every
k\in[1,j]
and primitive polynomial
f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})
such
that
a^{\prime}\cdot f^{\prime}
appears in the left-hand side of a
divisibility in
\Psi^{\prime}
, for some
a^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, and
\textup{LV}(f^{\prime})=y_{k}
, we have

\textup{M}_{f^{\prime}}(\Psi^{\prime})\cap\mathbb{Z}[y_{1},\dots,y_{k}]=\mathbb{Z}f^{\prime}
.
By definition of
\Psi^{\prime}
and since
a^{\prime}\cdot f^{\prime}
appears
in a left-hand side, there is a primitive polynomial

f(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})
and a scalar
a\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
such that
a\cdot f
is in the left-hand side of
some divisibility in
\Psi
, and
a^{\prime}\cdot f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})=a\cdot f(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
. Note that this
implies
a\mathrel{|}a^{\prime}
and
\textup{LV}(f)\not\in X_{1}
. We prove
that
\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}\cdot\textup{M}_{f^{\prime}}(\Psi^{\prime})\subseteq\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x:x\in X_{1}]
. Note that this inclusion implies
\Psi^{\prime}
in
increasing form. To see this, take
g^{\prime}\in\textup{M}_{f^{\prime}}(\Psi^{\prime})\cap\mathbb{Z}[y_{1},\dots,y_{k}]
. We have

\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}\cdot g^{\prime}\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x:x\in X_{1}]
, and thus there is
g(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)
such that
\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}\cdot g^{\prime}=g(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
. Since
\textup{LV}(g^{\prime})\mathbin{\prec}^{\prime}y_{k}
, we have
\textup{LV}(g)\mathbin{\prec}^{\prime}y_{k}
. Since
\Psi
is
increasing for
\mathbin{\prec}^{\prime}
, we conclude that
g\in\mathbb{Z}f
. Note that
(\mathbb{Z}f)[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x\in X_{1}]\subseteq\mathbb{Z}f^{\prime}
. Then
\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}\cdot g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}f^{\prime}
. Since
f^{\prime}
is primitive, we get
g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}f^{\prime}
. This shows
\textup{M}_{f^{\prime}}(\Psi^{\prime})\cap\mathbb{Z}[y_{1},\dots,y_{k}]\subseteq\mathbb{Z}f^{\prime}
, and the other
inclusion directly follows by definition of

\textup{M}_{f^{\prime}}(\Psi^{\prime})
. We conclude that
\Psi^{\prime}
is
increasing.

To conclude the proof of
4
,
let us show that
\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}\cdot\textup{M}_{f^{\prime}}(\Psi^{\prime})\subseteq\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x:x\in X_{1}]
. By definition of
\textup{M}_{f^{\prime}}(\Psi^{\prime})
,
this follows as soon as we prove the following three
properties:

1.

\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}\cdot f^{\prime}

belongs to
\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x:x\in X_{1}]
,

2.

\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x:x\in X_{1}]
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-module, and

3.

If
g^{\prime}\mathrel{|}h^{\prime}
is a
divisibility in
\Psi^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}\cdot g^{\prime}\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x:x\in X_{1}]
for some
b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, then
b^{\prime}\cdot h^{\prime}\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x:x\in X_{1}]
.

By definition of divisibility module,
\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}\cdot\textup{M}_{f^{\prime}}(\Psi^{\prime})
is the smallest set that
satisfies the three properties above, and therefore it
must be included in
\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x:x\in X_{1}]
.

Proof of

1
: By definition of
f
,

a^{\prime}\cdot f^{\prime}=a\cdot f(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
and

a\mathrel{|}a^{\prime}
, hence
\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}\cdot f^{\prime}=f(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
, and by definition of divisibility
module
f(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x:x\in X_{1}]
.

Proof of

2
: This follows directly
from the definition of divisibility module being a

\mathbb{Z}
-module. Indeed, substitutions preserve the notion
of
\mathbb{Z}
-module.

Proof of

3
: This property follows
from our definition of
\mathrm{S}_{f}(\Psi)
together with
the property (
IH3:
) and the
fact that
\Psi
has the elimination property for the
order
\mathbin{\prec}
(not to be confused with the
order
\mathbin{\prec}^{\prime}
, which does not guarantee the
elimination property). Consider a divisibility
g^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})\mathrel{|}h^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})
occurring in
\Psi^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
such that
b^{\prime}\cdot g^{\prime}\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x:x\in X_{1}]
. By definition of
\Psi^{\prime}
, there is a divisibility

g(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\mathrel{|}h(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})
in
\Psi

such that
g^{\prime}=g(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
and
h^{\prime}=h(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
. Also, by definition of

\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x:x\in X_{1}]
, there is a polynomial
\widehat{g}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)
such that
b^{\prime}\cdot g^{\prime}=\widehat{g}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
.

To conclude the proof, it suffices to show that
b^{\prime}\cdot g=\widehat{g}
. Indeed, since
g\mathrel{|}h
appears
in
\Psi
and
\widehat{g}\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)
, we then
get
b^{\prime}\cdot h\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)
by the definition
of divisibility module, which implies
b^{\prime}\cdot h^{\prime}\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)[\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\mathbin{/}x:x\in X_{1}]

by definition of
h
; concluding the proof.

Since
\widehat{g}\in\textup{M}_{f}(\Psi)
and
\Psi
has
the elimination property for
\mathbin{\prec}
, there are
linearly independent polynomials
h_{1},\dots,h_{\ell}
such
that the divisibilities
f\mathrel{|}h_{i}
appear in
\Psi

and there are
{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}
such that
\widehat{g}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\lambda_{i}\cdot h_{i}
. Thanks
to
Lemma

19
, we can arrange these
polynomials so that
\textup{LV}(h_{1})\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}\textup{LV}(h_{\ell})
. We write
c_{i}
for the coefficient
corresponding to the leading variable of
h_{i}
. Since

\textup{LV}(f)\not\in X_{1}
(stated earlier) and
\Psi
is
increasing,
{\textup{LV}(h_{i})\in\bigcup_{k=2}^{r}X_{k}}
holds
for every
i\in[1,\ell]
. From
g^{\prime}=g(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
and
b^{\prime}\cdot g^{\prime}=\widehat{g}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
we directly get
b^{\prime}\cdot g(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})=\widehat{g}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})
. Therefore,
(b^{\prime}\cdot g-\widehat{g})(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})=0
, implying that
b^{\prime}\cdot g-\widehat{g}
is either constant or has its leading
variable in
X_{1}
. This implies that
b^{\prime}\cdot g-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\lambda_{i}\cdot h_{i}
is either
constant or has its leading variable in
X_{1}
. Since the

\lambda_{i}
are non-zero, and moreover
\textup{LV}(h_{i})
is not
in
X_{1}
and
\textup{LV}(h_{1})\mathbin{\prec}\dots\mathbin{\prec}\textup{LV}(h_{\ell})
, we have
\textup{LV}(b^{\prime}\cdot g-\sum_{i=k+1}^{\ell}\lambda_{i}\cdot h_{i})=\textup{LV}(h_{k})
for every
k\in[1,\ell]
, and the coefficient corresponding to the
leading variable of
b^{\prime}\cdot g-\sum_{i=k+1}^{\ell}\lambda_{i}\cdot h_{i}
is exactly
\lambda_{k}\cdot c_{k}
.

We show by induction on
k\in[1,\ell+1]
, with base
case
k=\ell+1
, that
\alpha_{k}\cdot(b^{\prime}\cdot g-\sum_{i=k}^{\ell}\lambda_{i}\cdot h_{i})=b^{\prime}\cdot S(g,h_{\ell},\dots,h_{k})
, where
\alpha_{k}\coloneqq\prod_{i=k}^{\ell}c_{i}
, and
S(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
is short
for
S(\dots(S(f_{1},f_{2}),\dots),f_{n})
; e.g.,

S(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})=S(S(f_{1},f_{2}),f_{3})
.

base case
k=\ell+1
:

For the base case,

\alpha_{\ell+1}=1
and the equivalence becomes

b^{\prime}\cdot g=b^{\prime}\cdot g
.

induction step
k\leq\ell
:

we have
{\alpha_{k+1}(b^{\prime}\cdot g-\sum_{i=k+1}^{\ell}\lambda_{i}\cdot h_{i})=b^{\prime}\cdot S(g,h_{\ell},\dots,h_{k+1})}
by induction hypothesis.
Note that, from the left-hand side of this equation,
the coefficient corresponding to the leading
variable of
b^{\prime}\cdot S(g,h_{\ell},\dots,h_{k+1})
is

c_{k}\cdot\alpha_{k+1}\cdot\lambda_{k}
. Then,

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}\cdot(b^{\prime}\cdot g-\sum_{i=k}^{\ell}\lambda_{i}\cdot h_{i})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle c_{k}\cdot\alpha_{k+1}(b^{\prime}\cdot g-\sum_{i=k}^{\ell}\lambda_{i}\cdot h_{i})

\displaystyle\text{definition of}~\alpha_{k}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle c_{k}\cdot\alpha_{k+1}(b^{\prime}\cdot g-\sum_{i=k+1}^{\ell}\lambda_{i}\cdot h_{i})-c_{k}\cdot\alpha_{k+1}\cdot\lambda_{k}\cdot h_{k}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle c_{k}\cdot(b^{\prime}\cdot S(g,h_{\ell},\dots,h_{k+1}))-(c_{k}\cdot\alpha_{k+1}\cdot\lambda_{k})\cdot h_{k}

induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle S(b^{\prime}\cdot S(g,h_{\ell},\dots,h_{k+1}),h_{k})

\displaystyle\text{coeff.~leading var.}~h_{k}~\text{is}~c_{k}

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt\text{coeff.~leading var.}~(b^{\prime}\cdot S(g,h_{\ell},\dots,h_{k+1}))~\text{is}~c_{k}\cdot\alpha_{k+1}\cdot\lambda_{k}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle b^{\prime}\cdot S(g,h_{\ell},\dots,h_{k})

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826ptS(b^{\prime}\cdot f_{1},f_{2})=b^{\prime}\cdot S(f_{1},f_{2}),\text{by definition of }S\text{-polynomial}.

Thanks to the equality
\alpha_{k}\cdot(b^{\prime}\cdot g-\sum_{i=k}^{\ell}\lambda_{i}\cdot h_{i})=b^{\prime}\cdot S(g,h_{\ell},\dots,h_{k})
we just established, we conclude
that
\alpha_{1}\cdot(b^{\prime}\cdot g-\widehat{g})=b^{\prime}\cdot S(g,h_{\ell},\dots,h_{1})
. Moreover, from
\textup{LV}(b^{\prime}\cdot g-\sum_{i=k+1}^{\ell}\lambda_{i}\cdot h_{i})=\textup{LV}(h_{k})
we conclude that

\textup{LV}(S(g,h_{\ell},\dots,h_{k+1}))=\textup{LV}(h_{k})
, for every

k\in[1,\ell]
. Then, since
{g\in\textup{terms}(\Psi)}
and
the divisibilities
{f\mathrel{|}h_{1}},\,\dots,\,{f\mathrel{|}h_{\ell}}
appear in
\Psi
, by definition of

\mathrm{S}_{f}(\Psi)
, we conclude that

{S(g,h_{\ell},\dots,h_{1})\in\mathrm{S}_{f}(\Psi)}
. Recall
that
b^{\prime}\cdot g-\widehat{g}
is either constant or
has its leading variable in
X_{1}
. The same is true for

S(g,h_{\ell},\dots,h_{1})
, and we have
(\alpha_{1}\cdot(b^{\prime}\cdot g-\widehat{g}))(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}))=b^{\prime}\cdot S(g,h_{\ell},\dots,h_{1})(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}))
. From
(b^{\prime}\cdot g-\widehat{g})(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}))=(b^{\prime}\cdot g-\widehat{g})(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{y})=0
and
b^{\prime}\neq 0
we get
{S(g,h_{\ell},\dots,h_{1})(\boldsymbol{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}))=0}
. From the
property (
IH3:
), this can
only occur when
S(g,h_{\ell},\dots,h_{1})=0
, and so

\alpha_{1}\cdot(b^{\prime}\cdot g-\widehat{g})=0
. By
definition
\alpha_{1}\neq 0
, and therefore
b^{\prime}\cdot g=\widehat{g}
, concluding the proof
of (
3
).
∎

Proof.

The first statement of the claim follows
from (
IH1:
) and the definition of

\mu_{p}
(the reasoning is analogous to the one in the base

of the induction
of

4
). For the second

\mathbf{P}_{\!\!+}(\Psi)
. We provide a solution

for

\Psi^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})
modulo

. Let
\boldsymbol{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{j})

\alpha_{i}

case
p\nmid\alpha_{i}
for all

.

This case is relatively simple. Starting
from
y_{1}
and proceeding in increasing order of
variables, we compute

(

) by

\displaystyle h(b_{p,1},\dots,b_{p,k},\,y_{k+1})

\displaystyle\not\equiv 0

\displaystyle\pmod{p}

\displaystyle h\in\textup{terms}(\Psi^{\prime})\text{ s.t.}~\textup{LV}(h)=y_{k+1}.

(13)

With respect to the
h
above, let us write

h(b_{p,1},\dots,b_{p,k}\,y_{k+1})=c_{h}+a_{h}\cdot y_{k+1}
where

with respect to

(b_{p,1},\dots,b_{p,k})

. By definition of
\Psi^{\prime}
, the term
